# loke — Epics & Stories

**License:** Apache 2.0
**Version:** v1.3 (2026-09-23 — the build is green, 218 of 218 modules and 122 of 122 tests; the open
work is wiring, evidence and the 247 files the build has never touched. Backlog re-measured against
the tree throughout; every figure changed carries the date. See `TOOLCHAIN.lock` for the toolchain)

---

> **This is the project's only backlog.** `progress.json` was retired on 2026-09-19 and is archived at
> [`archive/progress.json.retired-2026-09-19`](archive/progress.json.retired-2026-09-19); its open
> items were re-verified and migrated as **Epic BG1**. Do not create a second backlog — the previous
> pair produced ID collisions where the same letter named different epics, and statuses that were four
> months stale.

## How This Document Is Structured

Epics are grouped into three layers: **Foundation** (the core engine that all modes share), **Platform** (the extensibility, UI, and infrastructure layer that applications build on), and **Application** (the user-facing modes and interfaces). Cross-cutting concerns span all layers.

Story sizing uses T-shirt sizes: **S** (< 1 day), **M** (1-3 days), **L** (3-5 days), **XL** (1-2 weeks).

Stories sourced from the platform requirements document are tagged with their R-number origin (e.g. `[R2.3]`) for traceability.

### The story table schema

Every story table has exactly four columns — `| Story | Size | Status | Summary |` — and every story
row has exactly four cells and a closing `|`. A literal `|` inside a cell is written `\|`. This is not
housekeeping: `scripts/check_backlog.py` parses these rows, the SUMMARY below is generated from them,
and `docs/claims.md` is derived from that. Before RV1.7a eight epics used a three-column table with no
status column at all, carrying status as a trailing `**[Done]**` inside the Summary cell, which is why
two careful parses of this file once disagreed on its own row count.

**Status vocabulary — closed.** The Status cell is exactly one of the following, and nothing else. The
cell is capped at 120 characters; rationale longer than that belongs in the Summary, after
`**Status detail:**`.

| Status | Means |
|--------|-------|
| *(blank)* | Not started |
| `**Done**` | Done. Optionally followed by ` — <short note>`, usually a date |
| `**Reopened**` | Was marked Done, exercised, and found false. Optionally ` — <short reason>` |
| `**Partial**` | Some of it is done and the rest is named. Optionally ` — <short reason>` |
| `⏸ On hold — <reason>` | Blocked on something that is not a story: a missing toke capability, hardware. *(reserved — no row currently carries it)* |
| `Blocked — <ID or reason>` | Blocked on a named story, or on a stated non-story reason |
| `Queued behind <ID>` | Ordered after another story rather than blocked by it |
| `Superseded by <ID>` | The work moved wholesale into another story |

**What a `**Done**` marker must carry (RV1.7).** A row whose subject is runtime behaviour is Done
only when its Summary names the evidence that exercised it: the test file that asserts it against the
real module, or the command that was run and what it printed. "Implemented" is not evidence, and a
test that only reads the source is not a test of the behaviour. Where the assertion was proven able to
fail, say so, because a test nobody has seen fail is the thing this backlog was full of. A hygiene row
(a sweep, a rename) names the detector that now reports zero. This is a rule for reviewers, not a check.
No script can tell whether a named test really asserts what the row claims, which is why RV1 exists.

`Spec done` was in this legend until RV1.7a and appeared on **zero** rows, so it has been removed
rather than kept as an unused value. Reinstating it means adding it here and to the `STATUSES` table in
`scripts/check_backlog.py` in the same commit — the checker fails on a status that is not in both.

> **⏸ On hold** stories are blocked pending a dependency becoming ready for development. See the hold notice on each affected layer for detail. **The toolchain hold was cleared on 2026-09-20** and the build has been green since 2026-09-22 — so the only remaining holds are hardware (LC1), a genuinely missing toke capability, or another story. **Re-surveyed twice on 2026-09-23, and the second survey closes the category: `std.zip`, `std.toon` and `std.encrypt` are present at the pinned commit, and `std.xlsx`, `std.pdf` and `std.ocr` all landed just after it. So NO story in this document is now held on a missing toke capability** — the four asks in `docs/toke-libraries-required.md` (PDF, zip, XLSX, OCR) are all satisfied, and `std.ocr` binds macOS Vision directly so the standalone OCR repository it proposed is unnecessary. What remains is a `TOOLCHAIN.lock` decision, not a gap. The only real holds left are hardware (LC1) and other stories. As of that survey **no story row carries `⏸`** — X8.5, the last one, was released when NC1.9 landed.

# FOUNDATION LAYER

> **Foundation layer — now in development**
>
> loke is built on **ooke** ([github.com/karwalski/ooke](https://github.com/karwalski/ooke)) — a lightweight CMS and web application framework written in the toke programming language. **The toolchain hold was cleared on 2026-09-20** and [`TOOLCHAIN.lock`](../TOOLCHAIN.lock) is the authority. **The build is green as of 2026-09-22: 218 of 218 modules compile, 0 errors, and `packages/browser/build/loke` links.** The suite runs 122 of 122 passing. Superseded figures, recorded so they are not reinstated: "58 of 199 modules, 291 errors" (2026-09-20) and "217 of 217" (2026-09-22). **What green does not mean:** the build covers four roots and **247 non-test `.tk` files in six trees have never been compiled** (F10.23), and only 26 of 122 test files import a production module (X7). Remaining F10 work: F10.8, F10.9, F10.12, F10.13, F10.15a, F10.16, F10.17, F10.20, F10.21, F10.22, F10.23.
>
> **Architectural impact of ooke:**
>
> | Previously specified | Replaced by |
> |---|---|
> | TypeScript (strict, ESM) | toke programming language |
> | pnpm workspaces monorepo | ooke build system (`ooke.toml`) |
> | Electron (browser mode app shell) | ooke native binary + web view |
> | Node.js runtime | ooke compiled native binary (C) |
> | ESLint / Prettier | toke linting and formatting tools |
> | Vitest test runner | toke test runner |
> | @electron/llm (in-process inference) | Native inference via ooke bindings |
> | Transformers.js (browser-side NER) | Local SLM via ooke native bindings |
> | compromise.js (JS NLP library) | NER via local SLM (language-agnostic) |
> | LanceDB (Node.js vector store) | Vector store via ooke native bindings |
> | better-sqlite3 / SQLCipher | SQLite + SQLCipher via ooke native bindings |
>
> What does **not** change: Ollama (REST API, language-agnostic), Presidio (Python sidecar via REST), MCP protocol (implemented in toke), the pipeline architecture, all functional requirements, and all acceptance criteria.

## Epic F2: Local Model Integration

*Enable loke to run small language models locally for privacy filtering, intent classification, summarisation, simple completions, and tiered inference — matching model size to task urgency.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| F2.1 | L | **Done** | Ollama service manager (auto-detect, start/stop via REST API, health check, model management — Ollama is language-agnostic, no change to functional requirement) |
| F2.2 | M | **Done** | Model capability registry (capabilities, benchmarks, task-to-model mapping — implemented in toke) |
| F2.3 | L | **Done** | MLX backend for Apple Silicon (native toke/ooke bindings or REST bridge to MLX — 8-9% faster than llama.cpp on Apple hardware) |
| F2.4 | M | **Done** | Native in-process inference via ooke bindings (replaces @electron/llm — same functional requirement: low-latency local inference without spawning a separate process) |
| F2.5 | M | **Done** | Local NER and embeddings (replaces Transformers.js — NER and embedding inference via local SLM through ooke native bindings or Ollama REST) |
| F2.6 | XL | **Done** | Tiered inference engine (three tiers: Interactive — fully in GPU/unified memory, 25–55 tok/s, models up to 10B; Considered — partial GPU offload via Ollama `num_gpu`/llama.cpp `--n-gpu-layers`, 5–15 tok/s, up to 70B quantised; Background — maximum offload with heavy RAM/disk streaming, 0.5–5 tok/s, 70B+ full precision; tier selected by F5 router based on request source, user flag, task classification, and AG1 latency tolerance; progress indicator for Considered and Background; tier benchmarked on first run per model) |
| F2.7 | L | **Done** | Background inference queue (sequential job queue for Background-tier requests; priority: user-initiated > agent-scheduled > evaluation; Interactive always preempts — Background job pauses and resumes; `loke queue list/cancel/prioritise/pause/resume`; persists across restarts; completion notifications; queue metrics fed into G4 dashboards) |
| F2.8 | L | **Done** | Hardware-aware model recommendations (profile RAM, GPU VRAM, unified memory, disk type/speed, CPU on first run; per-model viability assessment at each inference tier; recommendations shown in model selector with tier badges; refreshed on hardware change, new model added, or companion device paired; `loke models recommend` CLI; disk space warnings) |
| F2.8a | M | **Done** — 2026-09-26 | **Hardware detection is a stub** — Found 2026-09-24 closing T7.3. `core.models.hardware.detect()` logs "stub -- returning Mac defaults" and returns a literal profile (16 GB unified, NVMe 3000 MB/s, 8 cores, macos) on every machine, and `detectprofile()`, which `packages/browser/pages/installer.tk` calls, returns `0`. So every recommendation F2.8 describes is computed from constants. F2.8 is marked Done; its hardware-profile clause is not. Needs a real probe (sysctl `hw.memsize`/`hw.ncpu` on macOS) behind a function that returns plain fields, not an f64 across a module boundary (T-20). **Done 2026-09-26.** `detect()` measures, through `std.sys`: RAM (`totalramgb`, whole GB), core count (`cpucount`), architecture and platform. On Apple silicon the memory is reported as unified. GPU VRAM, disk type and disk speed are NOT measured, and stay 0 or `$unknown`, meaning unknown and never invented. `detectprofile()` returns `detect()` instead of the integer 0. **Evidence:** `tests/unit/models/test_hardware.tk` compares against an INDEPENDENT measurement, `sysctl -n hw.ncpu` and `hw.memsize` spawned by the test itself, so it is not circular. With the old stub, the core-count and RAM assertions both FAILED and `detectprofile()` then aborted the process with RT005. The serialiser assertions now use a fixed profile: they had pinned the stub's literals (nvme, 8 cores). |
| F2.9 | L | **Done** | Disk-streaming inference for extreme offload (Background tier only; load model layers from NVMe sequentially with overlapped I/O prefetch; decompose large model into per-layer shards in `~/.loke/models/shards/`; configurable RAM ceiling; requires NVMe — warns on SATA/HDD; graceful degradation if < 0.1 tok/s; actual tok/s displayed with cloud cost comparison; equivalent of AirLLM layer-by-layer execution via llama.cpp mmap/partial-offload) |
| F2.10 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **With Ollama stopped, the Ollama client reported it running, and listing models killed the process** — Found 2026-09-24, once `/installer` returned its value (LS1.1u). (1) `core.models.ollama.healthcheck` used `http.get`, which returns `$ok` carrying a nil response against a closed port (T-15), so it answered 1, "running", whenever Ollama was down. It was also declared `:i64` while all five callers bound the result as `$ollamastatus`. It now checks `shared.reachability` first and returns the sum type. (2) `listmodels` and `listrunning` parsed that nil body and crashed. They now return an empty list when the service is unreachable; empty rather than `$err`, because T-18 would read a returned `$err` as `$ok` in every caller. (3) The installer page read RAM from `hw.detectprofile()`, a stub declared `:i64` that returns 0, so the runtime aborted the worker with RT005 on every request. `hw.detect()` would have shown invented Mac defaults instead (F2.8a on the tests branch). RAM and free disk now come from `std.sys`. Measured: those functions return WHOLE GIGABYTES as integers whatever their interface says, and the page had printed free disk with `str.fromf64`, as 7.9e-323. **Evidence:** `tests/unit/models/test_ollama_offline.tk` (3 assertions, against port 59431; the old module SIGSEGVs) and `tests/unit/browser/test_installer_page.tk`. `/installer` answers 200 in `scripts/smoke_serve.sh`. A caveat found while writing the test: `shared.reachability` binds the port to decide, so a port below 1024 always reads as "listening" to an unprivileged process. |
| F2.11 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-25 | **The reachability probe answered "down" for loke while loke was listening** — Found 2026-09-25. `shared.reachability.localserviceup` decided by BINDING the port (`net.portavailable`). Measured: it answered "free" for 11430 while loke listened there, because toke's server binds the wildcard address with `SO_REUSEPORT`, so a second bind on loopback succeeds. Every toke and ooke server does this, so moke believed loke was always down. It worked for Ollama only because Ollama does not set that option. **Fixed:** a "free" answer is confirmed by a real TCP connect (`connects`, through `nc -z`, since `std.net` has no connect and `net.listen` segfaults, T-49). **Evidence:** `tests/unit/shared/test_reachability_connect.tk`, whose listener assertion failed under mutation, and `scripts/smoke_e2e.sh`. **Caveat, still open:** a port below 1024 always reads as in use to an unprivileged process. |

## Epic F3: Privacy & Anonymisation Pipeline

*Build the multi-layer defence-in-depth system that detects PII, replaces it with reversible placeholders, and restores originals in responses. All implemented in toke on ooke.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| F3.1 | M | **Done** | Regex PII detector in toke (emails, phones, SSNs, credit cards, IPs, AU TFNs/ABNs — 10MB/s target, zero external deps) **Re-verified 2026-09-24 (RV1.5).** The detection half stands. Emails, phones, SSNs, cards, IPs, TFNs and ABNs each have assertions against the real module in `tests/unit/privacy/test_regex_detector.tk` (44 assertions), `test_regex.tk`, `test_regex_spans.tk` and `test_patterns.tk`. **The "10MB/s target" is measured and missed (F3.1a, 2026-09-24).** This Done covers detection, not throughput. See `docs/metrics-baseline.md` for the figure, and F3.1b for the quadratic cost behind it. |
| F3.1a | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **Measure the regex detector's throughput against F3.1's 10MB/s target** — Filed 2026-09-24 by RV1.5. F3.1 has been **Done** with this target since it was written, and nothing has ever timed the detector. Run `core.privacy.regex` over a fixed synthetic corpus with a known PII density, at 1MB and 10MB, with the first run discarded as warm-up and N runs reported as a median and a range (VM1.3's method). Publish the result only in `docs/metrics-baseline.md`, labelled with the toke commit it was built against (X4.8). Either result is useful. Missing the target is a finding, not a failure of the story<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24. The target is missed.** Measured with `benchmarks/regex/run.py` (a new harness around `benchmarks/regex/bench.tk`, which times `core.privacy.regex.detect`) at toke `fedd48d`, 6 fresh-process runs per size with the first discarded. At 1 MB the median is roughly a twentieth of the target, and throughput falls as input grows. 10 MB never completes: all three attempts were killed by the OS. The figures, host, density and caveats are published only in `docs/metrics-baseline.md` ("Regex detector throughput"), and are not restated here. The cause is filed as F3.1b. |
| F3.1b | M | **Done** — 2026-09-27 | **The regex detector's time and memory grow with the square of the input, so a large prompt kills the process** — Filed 2026-09-24 by F3.1a. `docs/metrics-baseline.md` records peak RSS rising about ×3.7 for each doubling of input (141 MB at 0.25 MB, over 7 GB at 2 MB), and a 10 MB input is killed by the OS. In a single process, repeated 1 MB calls slow from 1.9 s to 7.3 s, because memory is not released between calls. The server is one long-lived process, so this reaches it. That is an availability failure and not a fail-open: a killed worker forwards nothing. The suspected cause is not yet verified. In `rundetection`, every match runs `str.substr(text;cursor;textlen)` to locate its span, which copies the rest of the text once per match, and `ph.applytotext` may repeat the pattern. Fix it and re-measure with `benchmarks/regex/run.py`. Acceptance: peak RSS grows linearly across 0.25 to 2 MB, and a 10 MB run completes.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-27.** The suspected cause was half the story. There were two allocations growing with matches × text length, neither ever freed. (1) The span search copied the remainder of the text per match; it now searches a window that grows fourfold. (2) `ph.applytotext` ran a global `str.replace` per ENTITY, so each repeat of a value copied the whole text and replaced nothing. Each distinct value is now replaced once, still globally, so no occurrence is missed. Fixing (1) alone halved memory and left it quadratic (82, 288 and 1 052 MB). A first attempt, an in-place byte search, was correct and 130× slower, because `str.charat` calls `strlen` per read (filed as T-55). **Measured** (`docs/metrics-baseline.md`, 2026-09-27, toke `0b0bc4e`, loke `0d29b5e`, VM1.3 method): peak RSS 40, 72, 138 and 276 MB at 0.25, 0.5, 1 and 2 MB, down from 141, 519, 1 971 and 7 185, so linear across that range. 10 MB completes in 15.9 s at 1.67 GB. Detections are identical to the old build at every size. **Both acceptance criteria are met.** **Still true:** time is superlinear (T-55), and 0.63 to 1.84 MB/s is far below F3.1's 10MB/s. `tests/unit/privacy` gains assertions that a repeated value is replaced at every occurrence, and that the window search finds a match past its first window. With the dedupe guard broken, six privacy suites fail. |
| F3.2 | M | **Done** | NLP NER detector (names, places, organisations via local SLM — replaces compromise.js JS library with toke-native or Ollama-backed NER) |
| F3.3 | L | **Done** | SLM-based NER detector (context-aware PII: "my boss John" vs "John Deere tractor" — via Ollama REST or ooke native inference) |
| F3.4 | L | **Done** | Presidio integration (180+ entity types, Python sidecar via REST — unchanged: Presidio is language-agnostic) |
| F3.5 | L | **Done** | Placeholder mapping and reversal engine (SQLCipher via ooke native bindings, relational consistency, secure deletion — replaces better-sqlite3) |
| F3.6 | L | **Done** | Privacy pipeline orchestrator (layer sequencing, conflict resolution, dedup, dry-run, visual diff, pluggable entity type registration) `[R4.1, R4.2]` |
| F3.7 | L | **Done** | Prompt template engine (versioned templates, parameter injection, no raw data concatenation, reviewable) `[R4.3]` — *spec remains valid, implementation moves to toke* |
| F3.8 | M | **Done** | Guardian system prompt (mandatory non-bypassable safety injection into every LLM call, version-controlled) `[R4.5]` — *spec remains valid* |
| F3.9 | L | **Partial** — the harness runs and asserts entity types; "100% coverage" was never measured | Privacy pipeline test harness (end-to-end: raw data + template → assert no restricted identifiers, guardian present, rehydration correct; 100% coverage) `[R4.9, R15.3]` **Re-verified 2026-09-25 (RV1.5).** The harness is real and runs in the suite: AD1.8 un-excluded it, and `tests/unit/privacy/test_harness_runner.tk` passes. X7.6 made it assert the entity types each case declares, both ways. The request path's "no restricted identifier leaves, and restoration is correct" is exercised end to end by `scripts/smoke_e2e.sh`. **"100% coverage" has never been measured,** and this document does not publish coverage figures (`docs/metrics-baseline.md`), so that half of the row is not Done. It is either measured or withdrawn. |

## Epic F3b: Configurable Multi-Layer Privacy Filters

*Extend the privacy pipeline to support multiple pluggable PII detection models — including the OpenAI Privacy Filter and other compatible token-classification models — with configurable layer ordering, per-layer confidence thresholds, and organisation-level trust policies. Users and organisations can compose their own defence-in-depth stack by choosing which filters run, in what order, and what to do when layers disagree.*

**Context:** The existing privacy pipeline (F3) uses regex (F3.1), local SLM NER (F3.2/F3.3), and Presidio (F3.4) as detection layers, orchestrated by the pipeline orchestrator (F3.6). This epic generalises the layer concept so that any compatible token-classification model — starting with [OpenAI Privacy Filter](https://huggingface.co/openai/privacy-filter) (Apache 2.0, 1.5B params, 50M active, runs on laptop, 128K context, 8 PII entity types, BIOES span decoding) — can be added as a detection layer alongside existing detectors. The key design goal is **composability**: organisations with strict compliance requirements can stack multiple independent models and require consensus before data leaves the device.

**Architectural principle:** Each privacy filter layer implements a common interface (`detect(text:str):@$piientity`). The pipeline orchestrator already supports layer sequencing and conflict resolution (F3.6). This epic adds: a filter registry, a layer configuration schema, trust/priority ordering, consensus policies, and the first external model integration (OpenAI Privacy Filter).

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| F3b.1 | L | **Partial** — reopened by AD1.7, see Status detail | **Privacy filter layer abstraction and registry** — Define a `$privacyfilter` interface type: `id:str`, `name:str`, `kind:str` (regex/ner/model/external), `detect(text:str):@$piientity`, `entitytypes():@str`, `isavailable():bool`, `confidence():f64`. Create a filter registry (`$filterregistry`) where layers are registered at startup. Migrate existing detectors (regex F3.1, NER F3.2/F3.3, Presidio F3.4) to implement this interface without changing their behaviour. Registry supports `register`, `list`, `get`, `remove`. Each filter declares its supported entity types and a default confidence score.<br><br>**Status detail (AD1.7, 2026-09-24):** was marked **Done**. `filter_registry.tk` and its unit test exist; nothing registers a layer and `pipeline.run` imports its layers by name (AD1.7, claims B7). |
| F3b.2 | L | **Partial** — reopened by AD1.7, see Status detail | **Layer ordering and trust configuration** — Add a `$layerconfig` type to `ooke.toml` and the settings UI: `[[privacy.layers]]` entries, each with `id`, `enabled:bool`, `priority:i32` (lower = runs first = higher trust), `mode:str` (detect-only / detect-and-mask / veto), `confidence_threshold:f64` (minimum confidence to accept a detection), `entity_filter:@str` (subset of entity types this layer handles, empty = all). Pipeline orchestrator reads layer config and executes filters in priority order. Layers with `mode:veto` can block the request entirely if PII is found above threshold. Default config: regex (priority 1), NER (priority 2), Presidio (priority 3, if available). Organisation-managed configs override user configs.<br><br>**Status detail (AD1.7, 2026-09-24):** was marked **Done**. `layer_config.tk` exists; no `[[privacy.layers]]` is read and `pipeline.run` runs a fixed order (AD1.7, claims B4). |
| F3b.3 | XL | **Partial** — reopened by AD1.7, see Status detail | **OpenAI Privacy Filter integration** — Integrate [openai/privacy-filter](https://huggingface.co/openai/privacy-filter) as a privacy filter layer. The model runs locally via ONNX runtime through ooke native bindings or via Ollama as a token-classification task. Supports the 8 entity types: `account_number`, `private_address`, `private_email`, `private_person`, `private_phone`, `private_url`, `private_date`, `secret`. Map OpenAI entity types to loke's normalised entity type taxonomy (F3.6 already has this). Handle BIOES span decoding to extract entity boundaries. Configurable operating point (precision/recall tradeoff via Viterbi transition biases). 128K context window means full-document scanning without chunking for most inputs. Register as `openai-privacy-filter` in the filter registry.<br><br>**Status detail (AD1.7, 2026-09-24):** was marked **Done**. The sidecar server exists (`packages/privacy-filter/server.py`, claims A5); `sidecar_client.tk` has no importer, so no request reaches it, and no operating point is applied (AD1.4, claims B9/B10). |
| F3b.4 | L | **Partial** — reopened by AD1.7, see Status detail | **Consensus and conflict resolution policies** — When multiple layers detect overlapping entities, define resolution strategies: `first-match` (highest-priority layer wins), `unanimous` (all layers must agree for entity to be masked), `majority` (> 50% of layers agree), `most-restrictive` (union of all detections — anything any layer flags gets masked), `confidence-weighted` (weighted average of confidence scores across layers, threshold determines action). Store the chosen strategy in `privacy.consensus_strategy` in config. Default: `most-restrictive` (safety-first). Each resolved entity records which layers detected it and their individual confidence scores for auditability.<br><br>**Status detail (AD1.7, 2026-09-24):** was marked **Done**. `consensus.tk` ignores its `strategy` argument and marks every detection `mask`: one behaviour, not four, and no importer (AD1.7, claims B1). |
| F3b.5 | M | **Partial** — reopened by AD1.7, see Status detail | **Per-entity-type layer routing** — Allow configuration to route specific entity types to specific layers. Example: `private_person` detection routed to OpenAI Privacy Filter (strong at contextual name disambiguation — "my boss John" vs "John Deere") while `account_number` and `secret` routed to regex (deterministic, no false negatives for known patterns). Config: `[[privacy.entity_routing]]` entries with `entity_type`, `preferred_layers:@str`, `fallback_layers:@str`. When preferred layers are unavailable, fallback layers are used. Unrouted entity types use the default layer ordering.<br><br>**Status detail (AD1.7, 2026-09-24):** was marked **Done**. `entity_routing.tk` exists with no importer; no routing configuration is read and every layer scans all text (AD1.7, claims B2). |
| F3b.6 | M | **Partial** — reopened by AD1.7, see Status detail | **Layer health monitoring and graceful degradation** — Each filter layer reports health status via `isavailable()`. The pipeline orchestrator checks health before each request. Never block the pipeline because a single layer is down — degrade gracefully with clear audit trail. Dashboard widget shows layer health status.<br><br>**Status detail (AD1.7, 2026-09-24):** was marked **Done**. Each layer's own `isavailable()` is checked by `pipeline.run`; the aggregating `layer_health.tk` has no importer and no HEALTHY/DEGRADED status is reported (AD1.7, claims B3). |

One fixed selection path exists in `packages/core/src/router/selector.tk` |

| F5.7 | M | **Done** | Model size escalation with user consent (non-blocking dismissible prompt when a larger model would improve quality but requires a slower tier: "This would benefit from Qwen-72B — ~30s instead of instant. [Use larger] [Stay fast] [Always for this task]"; preferences remembered per task type; capped at once per session per task type unless opted in; escalation acceptance rate feeds router learning via X5.3) |
| F5.8 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **`core.router.escalation` ignores its policy and cannot be stopped from escalating to a cloud provider** — three defects found 2026-09-24 while writing T2.4's test, all **latent**: `grep -rn core.router.escalation` over `packages/`, `src/` and `tests/` finds no importer at all. (1) **`shouldescalate` never reads its `policy` parameter** (`escalation.tk:41`) — measured, `$escalationpolicy{minconfidence:0.9;maxretries:0;allowcloudescalation:false}` gives a byte-identical answer to the default on all ten probed inputs, and all three fields of `$escalationpolicy` are read by nothing anywhere in the repository, so a configured policy is decoration. (2) **`escalationtarget` takes no policy at all** (`escalation.tk:64`), so `allowcloudescalation:false` cannot stop it: `llama3.1:8b` → `claude-3-5-haiku`, and every unrecognised model → `claude-3-5-sonnet`. There is no local rung and no refusal in the fallback. This is the component that most directly contradicts `canusecloud($restricted) == false`. (3) **The fallback swallows the top of the ladder** (`escalation.tk:76-79`): `escalationtarget("claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022")` returns itself, so a retry loop never changes model once it reaches the top — and `escalationtarget("")` also answers sonnet, which matters because an empty `modelid` is exactly what `providers.types.nocompletion` carries, so a FAILED completion would escalate to a cloud provider. Fix before anything imports this module: thread the policy into `escalationtarget`, honour `allowcloudescalation`, and make the unrecognised-model fallback refuse rather than default to the largest cloud model.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24.** All three fixed as prescribed, in the module and before any importer: (1) `shouldescalate(response;policy;attempts)` returns false once `attempts >= policy.maxretries`; `minconfidence` is **removed**, not honoured, because `$completionresponse` carries no confidence for it to compare against. (2) `escalationtarget(model;policy;level)` refuses a cloud rung (returns `""`) unless `policy.allowcloudescalation` **and** `core.router.sensitivity.canusecloud(level)`; local rungs stay available. (3) The fallback is `""`: an unrecognised model, the empty `modelid` of `providers.types.nocompletion`, and the top rung no longer answer sonnet. `tests/unit/router/test_escalation.tk` asserts each with PUBLIC/permissive controls on the same models. **Mutation-checked:** with the budget check removed, the sonnet fallback restored and the cloud gate disabled, 10 assertions go red. Still latent: nothing imports the module, so no request path escalates. |
| F5.9 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **The intent classifier's LLM path never used the model's answer** — Found 2026-09-24 (T2.5). `classifyfromobj` read Ollama's `message` with `json.str`, but `/api/chat` returns `message` as an **object**; `json.str` only accepts a quoted string, so the read failed and every reply fell back to the keyword result. Measured against the real module: a well-formed body whose content is `code_review` came back `chat`/`keyword` (**2 assertions red before the fix**). Invisible because the fallback is the keyword answer, so nothing looked wrong. Fixed with `json.getval`. Two more found while there: std.json turns an escaped `\n` into a bare `n` (T-44, filed upstream), so a reply ending in a newline was not recognised (**red with trim + equality**; now matched by prefix, safe because none of the eight names is a prefix of another); and a reply naming no task was labelled an `llm` classification of `chat`, a model opinion that was never given. It now falls back to the keyword result. `tests/unit/router/test_intent.tk` asserts all four. Not covered: a live Ollama round trip. |
| F5.10 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **The intent classifier sent every multi-line prompt to Ollama as invalid JSON** — Found 2026-09-24 fixing PL1.12. `classifybody` escaped the prompt with std.json's `json.enc`, which escapes only quote and backslash (T-45), so a newline went into the request raw; Go's JSON reader rejects that, and `classify` fell back to the keyword result for every multi-line prompt. Now `shared.jsonobj.escape`. `tests/unit/router/test_intent.tk` asserts the body has no raw newline; **red against the old code** (the companion `json.dec` assertion stayed green because std.json's reader accepts a raw newline, and the test says so). |
| F5.11 | M |  | **The Anthropic and OpenAI dispatch uses a call measured to send nothing** — Found 2026-09-25 (T-48). `toanthropic` calls `http.postheaders(http.client(url);"";bytes;headers)`, and `core.providers.anthropic` and `openai` use the same function. Measured over http against a local recorder, that exact shape sends NOTHING and takes the error arm, while `http.posturl` arrives. **Not measured over https,** because that needs a real provider key and would spend money; that is the user's call. It is consistent with what was seen on 2026-09-24: a cloud-routed probe under the developer's real HOME answered "provider request failed". If https behaves like http, no request has ever reached a cloud provider through loke. To close: verify against a TLS recorder, or with one real call the user authorises, then move to a client call that is measured to send, with headers. `http.posturl` carries no headers, so it cannot send an API key. |
| F5.12 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-26 | **The router picks a local model that is not installed** — Found 2026-09-25 by the end-to-end test. With `llama3:latest` the only model Ollama listed, the router chose `qwen2.5:7b` ("local-only (sensitivity: CONFIDENTIAL…) => qwen2.5:7b"). The fake model answers anything; a real Ollama would answer 404, and the request would fail as a provider error. The route should be chosen from `ollama.listmodels`, which now answers honestly when Ollama is down (F2.10). **Partial 2026-09-25.** The local dispatch (`toollama`) now checks `ollama.listmodels` before sending. If the routed model is not installed, it refuses with 503 `model_not_installed`, names the installed models, and audits the refusal. It does not substitute another model, because a routing decision taken outside the router is what GA1.7 removed. **Evidence:** 3 assertions on `isinstalled` in `test_pipeline_handler.tk`, and a second phase of `scripts/smoke_e2e.sh` that lists only `llama3:latest`. With the check disabled, that phase FAILED: the request was sent for `qwen2.5:7b`. **Remaining:** the selector should choose among installed models, so this refusal becomes rare rather than the default for anyone without `qwen2.5:7b`.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-26.** `core.router.router.routepreferinstalled` routes against the catalogue with every local model that Ollama does not list removed (`core.models.registry.installedonly`). It keeps that route only if it chose a LOCAL model; otherwise it routes exactly as before, against the full catalogue. So knowing what is installed changes which local model is chosen, and never whether a request goes to the cloud. Filtering alone would have: measured by mutation, a CONFIDENTIAL request with no catalogue model installed was routed to `claude-3-5-sonnet`. An empty list, when Ollama is down, routes as before. `page.api.pipeline` routes through it, and the dispatch's `model_not_installed` refusal stays for an installed set the catalogue does not cover. **Evidence:** `tests/unit/router/test_route_installed.tk` has 10 assertions. It shows chat goes to `gemma3:4b` when that is the only installed candidate, where the default is `qwen2.5:7b`. It also covers that nothing moves to the cloud with an uncatalogued model, an unfit model, or RESTRICTED data. It fails 1 assertion with the install list ignored, and 3 with the cloud guard removed. `scripts/smoke_e2e.sh` adds a phase with only `gemma3:4b` listed: the request is answered by `gemma3:4b`, and the fake Ollama receives it under that name. With the install list ignored, that phase FAILED with `model_not_installed`. **Still true:** an installed model the catalogue does not know, such as `llama3`, cannot be chosen, because the router has no quality or speed figure for it. |

## Epic F6: Storage & Audit Layer

*Persistent encrypted local storage for conversations, audit trails, PII mappings, configuration, and cache. Implemented in toke using SQLite + SQLCipher via ooke native bindings.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| F6.1 | L | **Done** | SQLite database schema (SQLCipher via ooke native bindings, WAL mode, foreign keys, numbered migrations with checksum verification, typed data access layer with parameterised queries) `[R3.1, R3.2, R3.6, R3.7]` |
| F6.2 | L | **Done** | Audit trail system (append-only, hash chain, tamper detection, export, SIEM forwarding; metadata only — never stores prompt/response content) `[R3.5, R4.7]` |
| F6.3 | M | **Done** | Vector store for semantic operations (via ooke native bindings — replaces LanceDB; prompt cache, routing examples) |
| F6.4 | L | **Done** | Secure ephemeral storage (mlock equivalent via ooke, secure wipe, auto-expiry, no disk serialisation) |
| F6.5 | M | **Done** | Database backup and restore (consistent snapshot via SQLite VACUUM INTO, timestamped naming, configurable retention, restore with validation) `[R3.8, R8.5]` — *spec remains valid* |
| F6.6 | M | **Done** | Persistent sync queue (enqueue/dequeue with retry and exponential backoff, survives restarts — implemented in toke) `[R3.4, R8.7]` — *spec remains valid* |
| F6.7 | M | **Done** | Namespaced settings store (typed key-value: string, number, boolean, JSON; `loke.*` prefix + application prefix; get/set/list API — implemented in toke) `[R3.3]` — *spec remains valid* |
| F6.8 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **A migration that failed was recorded as applied, and never retried** — Found 2026-09-24. A worker flagged it and it was confirmed by reading. In `core.storage.db.runmigrations`, each `mt db.exec(...) { $ok:v 1; $err:e $err(...) }` was a STATEMENT, so its value, the error included, was discarded. A migration whose SQL failed was then inserted into `schema_migrations` as applied. Every later migration ran against a schema missing its predecessor, and nothing reported it. The same applied to creating `schema_migrations` itself. **Fixed:** `applymigrations` stops at the first failure, does not record it, logs at ERROR naming the migration, and answers the failed version. `openstore` logs how many migrations are pending (`pendingmigrations`). That log line is the visible signal, because `runmigrations`'s own `$err` reaches a `!$lokeerr` match, which T-18 reads as `$ok` (LS1.1j). **Evidence:** six assertions in `tests/unit/storage/test_db.tk` using test-only versions 9001 to 9003: an invalid migration followed by a valid one. The failed version is reported, it is not recorded, nothing after it runs, both stay pending, and a valid one applies and is recorded. With the stop-on-failure branch disabled, 4 of them failed. |

| F7.4 | L | **Done** | toke MCP server in toke (compress, decompress, template, analyse — TOON+LLMLingua backend; toke is the natural implementation language) |

## Epic F8: Companion Device Support

*Extend local compute to nearby high-power devices over secure direct connections. Implemented in toke on ooke.*

> **F8.1–F8.4 compile now, and they still cannot work.** My earlier note here said `std.mdns` and `std.tls` declare types whose names toke cannot write, so these were blocked upstream. **That was wrong** — the compiler resolves those functions from its builtin table regardless of the `.tki`, and all four stories' modules now compile. The real position is worse for the feature: **every function in `std.tls` and `std.mdns` is a stub that ignores its arguments and returns 0** (T-11 in [`docs/upstream/toke-issues.md`](upstream/toke-issues.md)). There is no TLS and no mDNS on this toolchain.<br><br>Two specifics matter for F8.2's claim. `tls.tlsconfig` now takes `(cert, key)` only: the peer certificate and verify-peer flag that **are** the mutual-authentication half have nowhere to go, so "TLS 1.3 mutual auth" is not untested — it cannot be expressed through this API. And `tls.genselfsigned` takes a common name, so the 3650-day validity loke asked for is passed to nothing. `companion/channel.tk` therefore fails closed and logs why, rather than returning a zero handle a caller would treat as an encrypted socket. **F8.1 and F8.2 move from Done to reopened; F8.3 and F8.4 stay in RV1 for re-verification** since they use REST and do not depend on these primitives.

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| F8.1 | L | **Reopened** | Companion device discovery and pairing (mDNS via ooke, confirmation codes, security checks) |
| F8.2 | L | **Reopened** | Secure communication channel (TLS 1.3 mutual auth, certificate pinning, heartbeat — via ooke TLS primitives) |
| F8.3 | XL | **Reopened** — no importer, so no router integration and no privacy pipeline | Remote model execution (companion Ollama/Exo via REST, router integration, privacy pipeline applied) **Reopened 2026-09-24 (RV1.6).** `core.companion.executor` compiles and **nothing imports it**: a grep of `packages`, `src` and `tests` for an `i=…:core.companion` import finds none outside the companion directory itself. So the row's "router integration" and "privacy pipeline applied" are both false, because no request can reach this code. The exposure is nil for the same reason. It needs fixing before anything calls it: `executeconn` sends `req.prompt` as given, so a future caller that forgot to anonymise first would send raw text to the companion. `parseremoteresponse` also returns `ok:true` whatever it parses, so a malformed or error reply reads as success with empty text. No test exercises any of it. |
| F8.4 | L | **Reopened** — no importer; no cluster monitoring surface exists | Exo distributed inference integration (GPL-3.0 boundary maintained — REST API only, cluster monitoring) **Reopened 2026-09-24 (RV1.6).** `core.companion.exo` has **no importers**, found by the same grep as F8.3, so nothing monitors a cluster and nothing routes to Exo. The GPL boundary claim does hold, trivially: the module speaks only REST, and nothing links Exo. That is the one part of this row that is true. No test exercises it. |

## Epic F9: Model Evaluation & Benchmarking

*Give users objective, workload-specific data on every local model so they can make informed routing decisions — not trust generic benchmarks that don't reflect their actual usage.*

|-------|------|--------|---------|
| F9.1 | L | **Done** | Local model benchmarking suite (dimensions: throughput tok/s per tier, quality scored against reference outputs for user's task types, cost efficiency, memory footprint; built-in generic workloads plus user-custom workloads from usage history; runs in Background tier; results stored and comparable across models; triggered on model download, hardware change, user request, or monthly; `loke models benchmark <model>`; results feed F5.3 model selection engine and G4.3 provider scorecard) |
| F9.2 | M | **Done** | Model comparison A/B testing (`loke ask --compare "model-a,model-b" "prompt"` sends to both; browser mode split view; blind comparison option — names hidden until user rates; user rates both responses; cost and latency displayed; comparison data feeds router learning, benchmark database, and provider scorecard; `loke models compare-history`; bulk comparison in Background tier produces report) |
| F9.3 | M | **Done** | "Could this run locally?" advisor (after every cloud API response, local SLM assesses whether a local model at any inference tier could handle this task type with acceptable quality; if yes: subtle indicator with opt-in to local routing; weekly summary of cloud requests that could have run locally with estimated savings; only suggests local routing after benchmark evidence confirms quality; feeds G4.2 value realisation dashboard) |

# PLATFORM LAYER

*Infrastructure and extension points that make loke a platform applications can build on. These epics enable third-party applications to use loke's privacy, routing, and storage capabilities without forking the core.*

## Epic P1: Platform HTTP Server

*Local web server that applications can extend with their own routes, middleware, and views — alongside or independent of Electron/CLI modes.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| P1.1 | M | **Done** | HTTP server core (configurable host:port, default localhost:3000, optional TLS for local use, serves client directory as static assets with SPA fallback) `[R2.1, R2.2]` |
| P1.2 | L | **Done** | Versioned API routing (prefix `/api/v1/`, plugin-based route registration, applications register under own namespace) `[R2.3]` |
| P1.3 | L | **Done** | Composable middleware pipeline (enforced order: request ID → logging → CORS localhost-only → body size limit → timeout → auth pluggable → validation → error handling; named insertion points for app middleware) `[R2.4]` |
| P1.4 | M | **Done** | Standard response envelopes and request validation (success/error/paginated shapes, Zod schema validation on params/query/body, structured 400 errors) `[R2.5, R2.6]` |
| P1.5 | M | **Done** | Security hardening (CSP, X-Content-Type-Options, X-Frame-Options, Referrer-Policy, Permissions-Policy, HSTS in production, configurable per-route rate limiting with 429 responses) `[R12.1, R12.3]` |

## Epic P2: Platform Extensibility

*Plugin system and extension points so applications add domain-specific behaviour without forking the core.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| P2.1 | XL | **Done** | Plugin registration system (routes, middleware at named insertion points, health checks, navigation items, settings sections, views — single registration API) `[R2.3, R1.4, Section 6]` |
| P2.2 | M | **Done** | Startup and shutdown hooks (onBeforeStart, onAfterStart, onBeforeShutdown callbacks; applications run custom init/cleanup logic within the lifecycle) `[Section 6]` |
| P2.3 | M | **Done** | Extensible configuration modules (applications extend base Zod schema with domain-specific sections, additional env variables, validated at startup alongside platform config) `[R1.2, Section 6]` |
| P2.4 | M | **Done** | Anonymisation pattern registration (applications register entity types + detection regexes + confidence weights; merged into privacy pipeline at startup; hot-reload supported) `[R4.2, Section 6]` |
| P2.5 | L | **Done** | Extension point documentation and contracts (versioned interfaces, semver compatibility guarantees, breaking change policy, migration guides for major versions) `[Section 6]` |
| P2.6 | L | **Done** | Privacy pipeline hook API (applications insert custom stages into the privacy pipeline without forking core; hook slots: `before_ner`, `after_anonymise`, `before_restore`, `after_restore`; each hook receives the current pipeline state and returns a modified state; hooks registered at startup via `pipeline.register_hook(slot; f)`; hook execution order: registration order; errors in hooks are logged and skipped — never crash the pipeline; hooks visible in pipeline console A4.2; used by moke for schema-first and local compute injection) |
| P2.7 | M | **Done** | Custom LLM provider registration (applications register new provider adapters without forking `providers/dispatcher.tk`; adapter interface: `connect():bool`, `generate(prompt;opts):str`, `embed(text):@(f32)`, `health():bool`; registered via `providers.register(id; adapter)`; auto-included in router model registry; appears in model selector UI; capability declarations: streaming, tool-calling, embedding; used by moke to route data-analysis requests to schema-aware custom handler) |
| P2.8 | M | **Done** | Governance rule hook (applications inject custom rule evaluation into the governance gateway; hook receives `$eval_context` before risk classification; returns `?(str)` — `none` to proceed, a string reason to block; registered via `gateway.register_rule_hook(id; f)`; hook outcome logged in audit trail with hook id; used by moke to enforce schema-first protocol — block requests where raw data detected in prompt) |

## Epic P3: UI Platform

*Design tokens, theming, component primitives, and application shell — so applications get accessible, themed UI without building from scratch.*

# ACCOUNTABLE AI SYSTEMS LAYER

*Every AI capability executes within accountability controls. This layer sits between user intent and AI services, making every interaction governed, risk-classified, auditable, and owned. The goal is not to control the model — it's to control the system in which the model operates.*

## Epic G1: AI Governance Gateway

*Every AI request — browser mode, terminal mode, MCP tool call, or agent action — passes through a single governance gateway that enforces policy, logs decisions, and ensures accountability before anything executes.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| G1.1 | XL | **Done** | Governance gateway implementation (single mandatory entry point for all AI interactions; executes in order: authenticate → classify risk → check policy → filter data → log → route; `gateway.submit(request)` → `GatewayDecision`; < 100ms overhead; hot-reloadable config; F3/F4/F5 plug in as stages) |
| G1.2 | L | **Done** | Use case registry (built-in use cases: chat-completion, code-generation, code-review, summarisation, data-analysis, translation, classification, agent-task, mcp-tool-call; custom use case registration; each stores id, name, risk_level, purpose, approved_models, required_controls, owner; `loke use-cases list/add`) |
| G1.3 | L | **Done** | Risk classification and control gates (three tiers: Low → logging + guardrails + anonymisation; Medium + human-visible preview + cost confirmation; High + mandatory approval + explainability trace + enhanced audit; dynamic escalation when data sensitivity exceeds use case default; colour-coded indicator on every request) |
| G1.4 | M | **Done** | Accountability and ownership registry (business/technical/risk owner per use case; defaults to user for individual installs; owner metadata on every audit event; no use case active without assigned owner; `loke owners list`; changes logged) |
| G1.5 | M | **Done** | AI justification requirement (new use case registration requires: purpose, why AI vs simpler alternative, expected value, identified risks; built-in justifications pre-written; suggest simpler paths when AI is unnecessary; enterprise sign-off workflow) |
| G1.6 | M | **Partial** — reopened 2026-09-26: per-use-case and per-agent scope do not exist | Kill switch and fallback mode (global: `loke kill-switch on` stops all external AI calls; per-provider, per-use-case, per-agent; graceful fallback messaging; persisted across restarts; audit trail; enterprise remote trigger via policy push)<br><br>**Reopened 2026-09-26 by T10.1.** The Done marker was false for part of the row. Until T10.1 there was no per-provider scope at all; it now exists and is enforced before dispatch. Per-use-case and per-agent scope still do not exist (`isblocked` takes a use case and ignores it), and no enterprise remote trigger was checked. What is true: the global switch blocks every request (403), fails closed on an unreadable store, persists in settings across restarts, and is audited. |

## Epic G2: Transparency and Explainability

*Make every AI decision explainable after the fact — not just for compliance, but because users deserve to understand what happened with their data.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| G2.1 | L | **Done** | Decision trace system (captures: original input, PII detected, anonymisation applied, compression applied, risk classification, policy decisions, model selected + why, prompt sent, response received, deanonymisation, final output; `loke trace <id>`; JSON export; agentic workflows trace every step; 90-day default retention) |
| G2.2 | L | **Done** | "Why this output?" explanation generator (right-click → "Explain this" or `loke explain <id>`; generated locally by SLM from decision trace — no cloud call; plain language; source attribution for RAG responses; confidence indicators; thumbs up/down on the explanation) |
| G2.3 | S | **Done** | AI content disclosure (all AI responses marked with subtle clear indicator; categories: AI-generated/AI-assisted/AI-summarised/AI-translated; disclosure metadata on copy/export; enterprise-configurable; user-configurable visibility) |

## Epic G3: Operational Monitoring and Incident Management

*Treat AI like a production system with failure modes — because it is one.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| G3.1 | L | **Done** | Output quality monitoring (automated checks: hallucination detection, coherence scoring, relevance scoring; thumbs-down rate tracking per model/use case/period; baseline establishment over 30 days; drift detection alerts; provider comparison; feeds G4 dashboards) |
| G3.2 | L | **Done** | Incident management workflow (types: pii_leakage_suspected, policy_violation, quality_degradation, provider_outage, cost_overrun, agent_misbehaviour, security_concern; auto-created from monitoring or manual; severity: critical/high/medium/low; post-incident review template; `loke incidents list/create/resolve`; trends in G4) |

# AGENTIC AI

## Epic AG1: Agent Framework

*Lightweight AI agents that run small, well-defined tasks — on schedules, from triggers, or on demand — all governed by the same accountability controls as interactive use.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| AG1.1 | L | **Done** | Agent definition and registration (YAML/TOML format: name, description, schedule/trigger, model_preference, risk_level, permissions, max_cost_per_run, requires_approval, owner; auto-registered in G1.2 use case registry; deny-by-default permissions; validation before activation; `loke agents create/list/enable/disable`) |
| AG1.2 | L | **Done** | Agent scheduling and triggers (cron schedules; file change triggers; webhook triggers on local endpoint; MCP event triggers; manual via `loke agents run` or UI button; chained triggers A→B; debouncing with configurable cooldown; missed schedule policy; all triggers logged) |
| AG1.3 | XL | **Done** | Agent execution sandbox (permission enforcement: file read/write only to declared paths; MCP tool restriction; model restriction; cost limit enforcement with pause-and-alert; configurable time limit default 5 min; output staging with optional auto-commit for low-risk; no arbitrary network calls; agent isolation; all G1 governance controls apply) |
| AG1.4 | L | **Done** | Agent observability and debugging (status: running/idle/scheduled/paused/errored/disabled; run history with timestamp/trigger/duration/tokens/cost/outcome; live streaming log; step-through debug mode; G2.1 trace on every action; error handling with pause after N failures; metrics feed G4) |
| AG1.5 | L | **Done** | Agent templates and marketplace (built-in: daily digest, code review assistant, expense categoriser, meeting prep, documentation updater, security scanner; `loke agents install <template>`; full YAML editing after install; community export/sharing; templates include governance metadata) |
| AG1.6 | L | **Done** | Agent-to-agent communication (output triggers another agent; governed structured data handoff; handoff logged; circular dependency detection; declarative pipeline YAML: A→B→C; pipeline-level cost/time/risk limits; user notification on pipeline completion) |
| AG1.7 | M | **Done** | Agent latency tolerance and model tier selection (`latency_tolerance: instant\|patient\|background` field in agent YAML; `model_preference: largest-local` tells router to use most capable model achievable at declared tier even with heavy offloading; execution time budgeted with confirmation required for long-running agents; Background agents preferentially scheduled during low-usage windows; fallback to largest feasible model logged if preferred model unavailable) |
| AG1.8 | L | **Done** | Overnight batch processing pipeline (`loke overnight start` enters low-power full-utilisation mode for Background queue; user-configurable overnight window (e.g. 11pm–7am); full RAM/GPU allocation to Background tier during window; morning digest of all agent activity — results, findings, errors, costs; `loke overnight status/plan`; laptop power management — requires AC; interactive session preempts Background jobs within 10 seconds) |
| AG1.9 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-25 | **Agent lookup cannot say "not found", and duplicate ids are accepted** — Found 2026-09-24 closing T12.3. `core.agents.registry.find` returns an `$agentdef` through `i64!$lokeerr`, so its three callers — `scheduler.triggermanual`, `overnight` (job loop) and `agents.pipeline` (step loop) — take the first arm whatever happened (T-18): an unknown agent name is treated as found, and `find` itself loops `i>count` one past the end with `str.arrayget`. `get(reg;id)` (empty id when absent) and `has` now exist and are tested; move the three callers onto them. Separately, `register` accepts a second agent with an id already registered, and its `$err` arms (empty name, empty prompt) are unobservable for the same reason — return the registry unchanged plus a predicate, as `enable`/`disable` do. **Done 2026-09-25.** All three callers now ask `reg.has` first. `agents.pipeline` and `overnight` use it for the step and job check, and `scheduler.triggermanual` refuses an unknown id before it can run anything. `overnight.runbatch` and `pipeline.runpipeline` declared the registry as `i64`, a parameter that shadowed the module alias; both now take `$agentregistry`. Neither has a caller. `register` refuses a duplicate id, an empty name and an empty prompt by returning the registry UNCHANGED, which callers can observe, and logs why; two of its old error arms were not even wrapped in `$err`. Found on the way: `runpipeline` had never been called, and its first call died with RT005 nil dereference, because it read steps through the type-erasing `t.arrayget` (F10.22). It now uses a typed `.get`. **Evidence:** `tests/unit/agents/test_registry.tk` asserts that a duplicate is refused with the original untouched, that a nameless agent is refused, and that a two-step pipeline with an unknown agent is not ok, runs the known step and reports the unknown one as not found. With the old lookup and `register` restored, it failed. |
| AG1.10 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-26 | **The agent sandbox cannot decide a permission correctly, and nothing uses it** — Found 2026-09-25 closing LS1.1t. `core.agents.sandbox.checkpermission` returns `i64`, and both callers use it as a `bool`. `sandboxreadfile` and `sandboxhttpget` build their permission as a bare sum variant in value position (`$readfile(path)`), which T-13 records as producing a value that crashes. Violations were never recorded (its own comment). The module has no importer, so no agent is sandboxed today. Before any agent runs untrusted work: retype `checkpermission` to `bool`, build permissions through a constructor, thread the violation log back to the caller, and test a granted read, a refused read and a path outside the grant.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-26.** `checkpermission` takes a `$agentpermission` and returns `bool`, and reads the grants typed rather than through `str.arrayget`. Permissions are built by `readpermission`, `writepermission`, `toolpermission`, `getpermission` and `modelpermission`, never as bare variants. `readfile` and `httpget` return a `$sandboxstep` that carries the sandbox, so a refusal's violation reaches the caller. They replace the `str!$lokeerr` forms, which no caller could discriminate (T-18). **Also fixed, found while doing it:** grants were matched with `str.contains`. So a read grant for a directory granted any path containing it, including `<grant>/../outside/secret.txt`, a sibling `<grant>-evil/` and `/elsewhere<grant>`, and an http grant for `https://api.example.com` granted `https://api.example.com.evil.net`. Paths now match at a directory boundary and any `..` segment is refused. URLs match at a `/`, `?` or `#` boundary. `core.agents.executor` built its model permission with field syntax and declared three functions `i64` that return `$sandboxresult`; both are corrected. **Evidence:** `tests/unit/agents/test_sandbox.tk` has 24 assertions. They cover a granted read that returns the file, a refused read that does not read it and records one violation, a traversal read and a refused fetch recorded as the second and third, and the prefix and host-confusion cases. It fails 8 with the old `str.contains` matching restored. **Still true:** `core.agents.executor` has no importer, so no agent is sandboxed at run time yet. A granted fetch of a dead endpoint is unsafe to read, because `http.get` returns `$ok` with a nil body (T-15). |
