# NO-CUSTODY ARCHITECTURE

> **Why this layer exists**
>
> loke's original core path is: detect PII, substitute placeholders, send the redacted text to an
> external model. Peer-reviewed work establishes that this is not an effective privacy defence —
> large models infer and re-identify obscured entities from surrounding context (Staab et al.,
> ICLR 2024, arXiv:2310.07298; Staab et al., ICLR 2025, arXiv:2402.13846; RUPTA, arXiv:2407.11770;
> the Text Anonymization Benchmark, *Computational Linguistics* 48(4), 2022). Redaction reduces
> disclosure; it does not prevent identification.
>
> The architecture that does not depend on redaction working is one where **the model never receives
> the data at all**: loke sends a schema profile and the user's intent, the model returns an
> executable artifact, and loke runs that artifact locally against data that never leaves the device.
>
> **Before writing or repositioning any claim in this layer, read
> `docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md`.** It records what the external literature
> already establishes, what remains genuinely open, and — in §8 — a list of nine things loke must not
> claim because they are already published or are definitions rather than findings.
>
> loke already implements this, in one place, without having named it. moke's Insight Lab sends only
> column names, receives an analysis specification, and executes it locally
> (`packages/moke/src/ml/proposal.tk`, `packages/moke/templates/insight.tkt`). This layer makes that
> pattern the primary architecture rather than an accident, and demotes anonymise-and-send to an
> explicitly-labelled fallback for the cases that genuinely require prose in the prompt.

## Epic NC1: No-Custody Artifact Execution

*Make schema-out / artifact-back / execute-locally the default path. The model receives names, types and intent; it never receives rows.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| NC1.1 | M | **Done** | **Define the artifact contract** — Specify the closed operation set an artifact may express, building on the existing `$dashboard` DDL (`packages/moke/src/ddl.tk`) and the 15 operations in `packages/moke/src/compute.tk` (`opcount`, `opsum`, `opavg`, `opmin`, `opmax`, `opgroup`, `optimeseries`, `optopn`, `opdistribution`, `opcorrelate`, `oppercentile`, `opcountby`, `oplatest`, `opdistinct`, `opjoin`). The set MUST remain non-Turing-complete and MUST NOT include arbitrary code: toke exposes no sandboxing primitive (`process.tki` offers spawn/wait/kill only, with no jail, resource cap or syscall filter), so a closed op set is the only safe design. Write `docs/specifications/artifact-contract.md` with the grammar, the validation rules, and the explicit rejection criteria. |
| NC1.2 | M |  | **No-custody schema profile** — Build a profile emitter that carries column names, inferred types, cardinality, null-rate and row count, and **nothing else**. This replaces `packages/moke/src/hooks.tk::profiletotoon` and `packages/core/src/optimiser/profiler.tk::totoonschema`, both of which currently embed **three real sample values per column plus real min/max/mean** — so today's "schema-first" request ships real data. Acceptance: a profile built from a dataset containing a unique sentinel value contains that value zero times. |
| NC1.3 | M |  | **Wire the Insight Lab's proposal path** — The no-custody LLM path never executes: `packages/moke/templates/insight.tkt:897` posts `{columns: [...]}` while `packages/moke/pages/api/ml.tk:146-160` requires at least one of `schema_toon`, `num_cols`, `cat_cols` and returns 400 otherwise, so the client silently falls back to `heuristicSuggestions()`. Fix the contract, and fix `packages/moke/src/ml/proposal.tk`'s `if(i<n){...}` loops, which process only one element where `lp` was intended. |
| NC1.4 | L | **Done** | **Send the schema on the dashboard path** — `packages/moke/templates/dashboard.tkt`'s `buildPhase1Prompt(question)` sends only the question while telling the model *"You have access to this dataset"*. It does not. Pass the NC1.2 profile. Remove the instruction `Use realistic sample values that match the dataset schema` from both system prompts (`:1019` and `:1095`), and the equivalent clause in `templates/chat.tkt:2527`. |
| NC1.5 | M | **Done** | **Make fabricated values inadmissible** — Drop `normaliseDDL`'s ingestion of the model's `data` array into the render slot (`dashboard.tkt:448-468`) and the `Math.round(Math.random() * 900 + 100)` chart fallback (`:791-794`). A card with no locally-resolved value MUST NOT display a number. |
| NC1.6 | M | **Done** | **Provenance state on every card** — Render one of `resolved` / `unresolved` / `no-data` per card. `renderMetricCard` (`dashboard.tkt:714`) currently emits `→ computed locally` **unconditionally**, including on values the model invented; the only honest signal today is a `console.info` no user sees. `resolveQueries` MUST record why each `continue` fired so the reason can be surfaced. |
| NC1.7 | L |  | **Promote local execution into core** — `packages/moke/src/compute.tk::execute()` is a complete local query engine with no production caller. Make it (or a core equivalent) the execution target for artifacts, and add a second backend using toke's parameterised SQL (`std.db`'s `db.many` with bound parameters) so generated queries can run against the local store without string interpolation. |
| NC1.8 | M |  | **Demote anonymise-and-send to a labelled fallback** — Keep the path for tasks that genuinely need prose, but make selecting it explicit, record it as a custody event (DA1), and state its residual risk at the point of use. Update the pipeline so no-custody is attempted first and the fallback is a deliberate, logged decision rather than the default. |
| NC1.9 | M | **Done** | **Fail closed** — The defect is in **core, not the browser**: `packages/core/src/privacy/pipeline.tk:568` initialises `anonymisedtext` to the raw input and `:582` reassigns it from `anonymisetext(text; deduped)`. When the NER and Presidio layers are both unreachable the pipeline logs degraded mode (`:392`, `:523`, `:540`) and continues with an empty entity list, so `anonymisetext` returns the input unchanged and `packages/browser/pages/api/pipeline.tk:125-126,171` transmits it — while still reporting an `entities_found` count and a sensitivity label. A filter that fails open is worse than one that fails closed. Add a health gate: when no detection layer is healthy, return 503 and never dispatch. Fixing it in core fixes every caller at once; fixing it in the browser handler would leave the CLI path open.<br><br>**Status detail:** **Done** — `027e383`. The gate is a plain bool (`issafetoforward`), not a result, because T-18 makes a refusal returned as `$err` read as success to every caller. Asserted in `tests/unit/privacy/test_fail_closed.tk`, not by inspection |
| NC1.10 | L |  | **Bound the query-shaped channel** — A returned artifact is itself an information channel. An adversary able to shape queries and observe results across several turns can extract cell values a piece at a time, including by binary search, without ever being sent a row. So "the model never receives data" is true at the byte level and not necessarily at the information level, and **no published benchmark tests this** (`docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §11). Bound it: per-session limits on result cardinality and on repeated near-identical predicates, detection of value-probing query sequences, and disclosure accounting that counts *results returned to the model* as well as bytes sent. Until this is bounded, the no-custody claim must be stated with the limitation attached. |
| NC1.11 | S | **Done** | **Correct the architecture documents** — `docs/features-moke.md:200` states the schema is sent on the dashboard path; it is not. Update `docs/architecture.md` so the pipeline diagram shows the no-custody path as primary and the redaction path as fallback. |

## Epic DA1: Disclosure Accounting

*Record what actually left the device on every external call. Today nothing does.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| DA1.1 | M | **Done** | **Define the disclosure record** — Per external call: bytes transmitted, estimated tokens, field/column names disclosed, entity types and counts, whether any real data values were included, a custody flag (`no-custody` / `redacted-data` / `raw`), model and provider, and the correlation ID. This is the substrate every later claim depends on. |
| DA1.2 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **Create the missing metrics table** — `packages/core/src/metrics/collector.tk` writes to `metrics_raw`, which **no migration creates** (`packages/core/src/storage/migrations.tk` defines only `schema_migrations`, `settings`, `audit_log`, `placeholder_maps`, `sessions`), so `record()` fails at runtime. Add the migration, extend the schema with the DA1.1 fields, and fix `summarise()`'s hardcoded `avgsavingpct:0.0` (`collector.tk:91`).<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24.** `metrics_raw` is migration **20**, indexed by `timestamp` at 23 because every summary query filters on it. Found by the same sweep as M1.7's three, and it had not been connected to them because it belonged to a different epic. Columns follow what `core.metrics.collector.record` actually writes — counters INTEGER, costs REAL, `savings_by_stage` TEXT — and `timestamp` is INTEGER rather than the TEXT `datetime('now')` used elsewhere in this schema, because `event.timestamp` is an i64 and `summarise` does arithmetic on it. That inconsistency is real and deliberate: changing it would change what `summarise` returns. **Worth reading next to LS1.1q:** `collector.record` built a 15-placeholder INSERT against a table that did not exist, so no metric event this project ever emitted was stored, while `eval/bench.tk` reported 0 ms for every benchmark because the clock returned 0. Between them, nothing measurable was being measured. DA1.3 (wire the collector) is the next step and is still open. |
| DA1.3 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-26 | **Wire the collector** — Nothing anywhere calls `collector.record`. Call it on every external dispatch. Acceptance: after one request, exactly one row exists with non-null disclosure fields.<br><br>**Partial 2026-09-24.** `page.api.pipeline.recordmetric` writes one `metrics_raw` row through the new `core.metrics.collector.insert` from the two places a dispatched request can end: `completed` (a provider answered) and `providerfailed` (it was called and did not). A budget stop, policy deny or refused route writes none, because nothing left the process (disclosure-accounting spec §2.1). A cache hit writes a row marked `cache_hit=1` (added by DA1.6). That row is a savings-ledger entry, not a disclosure record (§6.3 P3). The row carries request id, unix timestamp, model, provider, whether the provider is local, provider-reported tokens, the router's cost, and the entity count as both found and blocked. `tokens_saved` and `cost_saved` are 0 because nothing on this path optimises. `insert` does not forward the event to external governance endpoints the way `record` does: `record` passes an i64 `extconfig` to a function expecting `$externalconfig`, so a 0 there is a null struct that gets dereferenced. **Evidence:** `tests/integration/test_metrics_dispatch.tk` (24 assertions as of DA1.6). After one answered dispatch there is exactly one row, with model, provider, `pii_found`, `pii_blocked`, `routed_local`, tokens and timestamp asserted. After one failed dispatch there is exactly one row too. With the two `recordmetric` calls removed, 14 assertions fail. `scripts/smoke_serve.sh` passes. **What remains:** the acceptance says "disclosure fields", and DA1.1 defines those as bytes transmitted, custody flag, field names disclosed and entity types. `metrics_raw` holds none of them, and the spec (§6.3 P3) says it must not become their home. They belong in `disclosure_log`, which does not exist. That is DA1.7.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-26, through DA1.7.** The disclosure fields now have their own table, as spec §6.3 P3 requires. After one request through the real server, `scripts/smoke_e2e.sh` finds exactly one `disclosure_log` row. Its bytes transmitted, custody flag, entity types, model, provider and correlation id are non-null, and its `bytes_body` is asserted against the payload the model received. Field names disclosed are `''` on this path: browser chat sends no dataset. The row never had been written by the running server before GA5.29, and neither had `metrics_raw`. |
| DA1.4 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **Typed stage records** — `packages/moke/src/console_log.tk`'s `$logentry{stage, status, detail, durationms, ts}` already rides along in the pipeline response and already reports column counts, but `detail` is free text. Add typed disclosure fields so the per-stage record is machine-readable rather than a string.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24.** `$logentry` now carries `disclosure:$disclosure` with the ten fields in spec §6.2 S1: `custody`, `bytes_body`, `bytes_schema_profile`, `tokens_estimated`, `tokeniser_id`, `fields_disclosed_count`, `entities_total`, `real_values_included`, `results_returned_cells` and `artifact_verdict`. `tojson` emits them as JSON numbers, booleans and strings (S3). A field a stage did not measure is `null`, never 0 (S5), and toke has no null, so it is carried as a -1 or "" sentinel. `append` is unchanged and records all nulls. `appendwith` takes a typed record. `page.moke.api.pipeline` fills the two fields this tree can measure today: `fields_disclosed_count` on `schema_extract`, from the profile's column count, and `entities_total` on `response`, read as a number from loke's `entities_found`. That one stays null if the field is missing. **Evidence:** `packages/moke/tests/test_console_log_disclosure.tk` (14 assertions) runs against the real `moke.consolelog`. It fails 13 with the disclosure object removed from `tojson` and the old quoting restored. All 36 moke tests pass. **Not done:** S2, which generates `detail` from the typed fields rather than alongside them, so `detail` is still hand-written prose. The page-level wiring is also not exercised by a test, because `post` cannot be called from one (T-16). The other eight fields need NC1 or DA1.7 before any stage can measure them. |
| DA1.5 | S | **Partial** | **Surface it** — Show disclosure per request in the pipeline panel and in aggregate on the savings view, replacing the hardcoded percentages currently in `packages/browser/templates/savings.tkt:138,225` and `packages/moke/templates/chat.tkt:2764`.<br><br>**Status detail:** **Partial — the fabrications are gone; the real figures need DA1.2.** `packages/browser/templates/savings.tkt` was 238 lines reporting thirteen invented numbers under "Cost & token savings / Today": $1.47 saved, 38% cache hit rate, 1.31x compression, 14 620 chars removed, 24% average prompt reduction, 142 optimised requests, and a per-provider table with costs to the cent for 142 requests that never happened. The page had **no data source at all** — no fetch, no storage read — and directly contradicted `metrics-baseline.md`, which says no cost, cache or token-reduction figure has been measured. Every figure now renders as unmeasured with the story that would produce it. The chat.tkt:2764 reference in this story is stale; that code is the locally-computed series path and is correct |
| DA1.6 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **Replace the stubbed metrics module** — `packages/core/src/audit/metrics.tk:37-87` returns zeros from every function and `""` from `export()`, while ignoring the `store` and `period` arguments it is passed. Implement against DA1.2.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24.** Every function now reads `metrics_raw`. `summary` (requests, tokens saved, cost saved, entities redacted, local ratio, cache hit rate), `byprovider` (cache hits excluded), `tokensavingsseries` (one point per UTC day that has rows, so a gap stays a gap) and `cachestats` are all period-filtered through `periodstart` (`all`, `24h`, `7d`, `30d`, `90d`). An unknown period is refused, not treated as `all`. `export` produces CSV or JSON and refuses any other format. Each result-returning function has a plain twin (`summaryfrom` and so on), because a caller matching the result always takes the first arm (T-18). Cost is summed as integer micro-dollars. Migration **29** adds `metrics_raw.tokens_out`, because the table had no output-token column and `byprovider` would otherwise have invented one. `$cacheinfo` loses `totalentries` and `avgsimilarity`: the table records neither, and returning 0 for them was the stub's behaviour under another name. It had no callers. `page.api.pipeline.cachedreply` now writes a row marked as a hit, without which the hit rate would be 0% by construction. **Evidence:** `tests/integration/test_audit_metrics.tk` (29 assertions) seeds rows through `collector.insert` with fixed timestamps. With the module's queries pointed at a missing table, which is the stub's behaviour, it fails 9 assertions and then aborts. `tests/integration/test_metrics_dispatch.tk` adds the cache-hit row and `tokens_out`, and fails 3 assertions with that insert removed. **Not done here:** `/api/savings` still returns a constant string and calls none of this. That is DA1.5. |
| DA1.7 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-26 | **Create and write `disclosure_log`** — Filed 2026-09-24 by DA1.3. `docs/specifications/disclosure-accounting.md` §6.3 P1/P2 puts the per-dispatch disclosure record in its own table, one row per dispatch on `correlation_id`, written at the same call site as `audit_log` in one transaction, with a non-null value in every reserve field of §5.1 to §5.4 (G3). No migration creates it and nothing writes it. So "bytes transmitted" (octets of the request body as handed to transport), the custody flag, field names disclosed and entity types are recorded nowhere. `toanthropic` and `toollama` build the body and could measure `bytes_body` themselves. Custody has to be observed rather than declared (C1 to C3), which needs NC1.2's sentinel check. Until that exists, the honest value on the redact-and-forward path is `redacted-data`, with `custody_check = not_run`.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-26.** Migration **31** creates `disclosure_log` with every column of spec §5.1 to §5.4 and §5.6, `correlation_id` UNIQUE, and a CHECK that a token estimate names its tokeniser (A8). The §5.5 results-returned columns are left out, because the no-custody path that fills them does not exist yet (NC1). `core.audit.disclosure.reserve` INSERTs the row in state `dispatched`. `complete` moves it to `completed` or `failed`, only from `dispatched`, so an outcome is written once. `page.api.pipeline` reserves in `toollama` and `toanthropic` immediately before the body is handed to transport. If the reserve write fails, the request is refused with a 503, audited as `llm_request_refused`, and nothing is sent. `completed` and `providerfailed` complete the row. The correlation id is the request id, which is also `audit_log`'s. `bytes_body` is `str.len` of the exact string given to transport, which counts octets. `bytes_question` is the escaped question within it. Custody is `redacted-data` with `custody_check = not_run`. `tokens_source` is `provider_reported` only when the provider reported a count, and NULL otherwise. No estimate is invented. `reserve` takes its facts as parameters rather than a record: a record type pushed `page.api.pipeline` past toke's 64-struct-type cap (T-54). **Evidence:** `tests/unit/audit/test_disclosure_log.tk` has 37 assertions against the real migration, covering lifecycle, the once-only outcome, the duplicate reserve and A8. It fails with the state guard removed from `complete`. `scripts/smoke_e2e.sh` runs the real server against the fake Ollama. It asserts one completed row whose `bytes_body` equals the octets the model received (A7), that the test address appears in no column (A10), and that a refused request writes no row. It fails with `bytes_body` measured from the question instead of the body, and with the reserve call removed. **Not done:** custody is passed rather than observed (C1 to C3, needs NC1.2). The abandoned sweep (L2/A4), the reserve sharing one transaction with the `audit_log` insert (P2), and `bytes_headers`, jurisdiction and model version remain; they are DA1.9. Writing this row found GA5.29. |
| DA1.8 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **The moke console log was invalid JSON in every pipeline response** — Found 2026-09-24 by DA1.4. `moke.consolelog.tojson` wrapped `shared.jsonobj.escape(...)` in a second pair of quotes, but `escape` already returns the string quoted. So every stage, status and detail went on the wire as `"stage":""schema_extract""`, inside the `console_log` field of every `/api/moke/pipeline` response, success and error alike. Fixed by using `escape`'s own quotes. **Evidence:** `packages/moke/tests/test_console_log_disclosure.tk` asserts `"stage":"schema_extract","status":"ok"` and that no doubled quote appears anywhere in the record. Both fail with the old quoting restored. |
| DA1.9 | M | **Partial** — (1) done; (2) to (4) open | **The rest of the disclosure lifecycle** — Filed 2026-09-26 by DA1.7. (1) A row left in `dispatched` past the request timeout is swept to `abandoned` and the abandoned count is reported (spec L2, L4, A4). (2) The reserve and the `audit_log` insert share one transaction (P2). Today the reserve is written before transport and the audit row after the response, so a crash between them leaves a disclosure without an audit row. (3) `bytes_headers` needs a transport that reports the octets it wrote. `model_version` needs VM1.2. `jurisdiction` and `retention_class` need a provider registry that does not guess (U5). (4) `session_id` is `none`, because the browser endpoint has no session.<br><br>**(1) done 2026-09-27.** `core.audit.disclosure.sweepabandoned(timeoutseconds)` moves every row still `dispatched` past the timeout to `abandoned`, with outcome `timeout`. It does not delete it (L2), and the bytes sent stay on the record. `abandonedcount` reports the count (L4). `page.api.pipeline.post` sweeps at five minutes on every request, and an abandoned row cannot later be completed. **Evidence:** `tests/unit/audit/test_disclosure_log.tk` gains 13 assertions: exactly the old unanswered row is abandoned, while a recent dispatch and a completed one are untouched; the count is reported; a second sweep finds nothing new. It fails 3 with the age condition removed. **Open:** (2) one transaction with `audit_log`, (3) `bytes_headers`, `model_version`, jurisdiction and retention, (4) `session_id`. |

## Epic EM1: Exposure Metric

*A disclosure measure that is defined, computed and validated — not asserted. Depends on DA1 and research spike RS2.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| EM1.1 | M | **Done** | **Specify the metric on established axes** — Adopt the three axes already established for disclosure risk — **singling out, linkability, inference** — each as a *control-corrected* rate, rather than inventing a scheme (`docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §4). A vector, never a weighted-sum scalar with hand-chosen weights. Quasi-identifier *combination* presence is the strongest single component; presence of any real value is a **gate, not a summand**; bytes and tokens are the strawman baseline to beat, not a privacy measure. State the threat model per component — mixing provider-side linkage, third-party analytics exposure and model memorisation into one number is the specific error the consensus literature warns against. If a single display number is needed, present it explicitly as a lossy projection of the validated vector. |
| EM1.2 | L |  | **Validate the metric, do not assert it** — In the order of persuasiveness set out in `docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §7: (a) **dose-response** — inject disclosure of known magnitude and show the metric scales with the dose; (b) **Spearman ρ against attack success across ≥30–50 *configurations*** (arm × dataset × model × redaction level, not individual requests) with bootstrap intervals; (c) against **two structurally different attacks** — linkage and attribute inference — since validating only against the attack the metric was built around is circular; (d) reported as **TPR at low FPR on a log ROC**, not AUC, because one confident identification is the breach. Skip membership inference against model weights: prompts to a frontier API are not training data under standard retention, and reaching for it looks like instrument-shopping. If the metric does not correlate, report that. |
| EM1.3 | M |  | **Comparative measurement across arms** — Report exposure for the three arms of CB1 (data-in-prompt, schema-only, full no-custody) on the same workload, so the architectural claim is quantified as *relative reduction*, never as elimination. |
| EM1.4 | S |  | **Surface through the Scorecard pattern** — `docs/design-principles.md:145` already defines an ambient, queryable scorecard tracking "privacy, cost, compliance, local ratio". Exposure belongs there, not in a warning. |
| EM1.5 | S | **Done** | **State the limits** — Publish, alongside the metric, what it cannot measure: inference from context, provider-side retention and training, and correlation across accounts. The metric bounds disclosure, not consequence. |

## Epic PL1: Placeholder Correctness and Linkage

*Fix a live restoration bug, then make the consistency decision deliberately. Depends on research spike RS1.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| PL1.1 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-23, verified by linking not by inspection | **Fix entity collision** — Three uncoordinated generators became one. `ner.makenerplaceholder` and `ner_local.labeltoplaceholder` both **hardcoded `_NER_1]`**, so every NER-detected PERSON got the identical surrogate; `presidio.tk` generated one and never recorded it; and `regex.tk` built a map the `$piidetectionresult` had no field to carry, making the repo's only `addentry` call site dead. Now: layers detect only, and `ph.assignplaceholders(entities;requestid)` runs once in `pipeline.run` between `dedupentities` and anonymisation, indexing each entity as `1 + (count of earlier entities of the same type)` — one generator, one counter, so a fourth layer cannot reintroduce the collision. Salted with the request id, which also closes the injection vector (a user typing a literal `[PERSON_1]` used to get a different person's real name back) and answers PL1.3's per-request arm. `ph.applytotext` is now the single substitution loop and orders **longest value first**: given `Bob` and `Bob Jones`, shortest-first left `[PERSON_1] Jones` — half a name replaced and the surname forwarded in the clear. **The defect, before the fix, quoted from the test:** `[PERSON_NER_1] reviewed it and [PERSON_NER_1] approved it` restored to `Alice Smith reviewed it and Alice Smith approved it` — Bob Jones erased and his action attributed to Alice. `tests/unit/privacy/test_placeholder_collision.tk` holds it, importing the real modules |
| PL1.2 | S | **Done** — already swept by LS1.1g; verified 2026-09-23 | **Fix the off-by-one in restore** — `placeholder.tk:46` uses `if(i>len){br}` where `len=str.arraylen(...)`, reading one index past the end. The same `if(i>n){br}` idiom appears in `packages/core/src/eval/bench.tk:85,128,171` and `privacy/test_harness.tk:181,190,229`; fix them together. |
| PL1.3 | M |  | **Decide scoping deliberately** — Placeholder indices are ordinal *within a single request* and shared across entity types (`makeplaceholder(entitytype, index)` called with the whole array's length: `regex.tk:85`, `presidio.tk:166`), so the same value gets different tokens in different prompts. Choose between stable-per-entity tokens (better coherence, introduces a cross-prompt linkage channel) and per-session salted tokens (defeats it, costs cross-session continuity), implement the choice as a documented default with the surrogate *style* — opaque token versus type-consistent realistic value — as a second explicit axis. Note the defence is not itself novel: published work already scopes mappings per conversation (`docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §2). |
| PL1.4 | L |  | **Measure the residual** — Run the five-arm scoping ablation specified in `docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §6 (persistent global / per-session / per-request / no-substitution / **random re-pairing control**), crossed with surrogate style. Report **control-corrected linkage advantage** against the re-pairing arm — never raw accuracy, since type-consistent surrogates leak structure and inflate the naive chance rate — with Wilson intervals, plus TPR at 1% and 0.1% FPR. The headline is the residual after the defence. Framing constraint: the *existence* of a linkage channel from stable surrogates is a definition, not a finding; only the magnitude, the scoping frontier and the residual are results. |
| PL1.9 | S | **Done** | *Done 2026-09-23.* **`dedupentities` was order-dependent, and the old rule leaked** — `overlapsbetter` dropped a candidate on mere span overlap, evaluated only against the prefix kept so far, so the same two detections in different arrival orders produced different entity lists. Worse, measured: "Bob" (0.95) overlapping "Bob Jones" (0.78) discarded the **longer** entity, and the anonymised prompt was `[PERSON_…] Jones signed.` — the surname forwarded to the provider in the clear. A second leak path, independent of the one PL1.1 closed. Replaced by `strictlyshadows` + `shadowedbyany`, evaluated over the **whole** input list: a candidate is dropped only when another entity's span covers it, its value **strictly contains** the candidate's, and it is at least as confident. Value containment is the load-bearing part — dropping an entity means nothing substitutes its value, so it is only safe when a survivor's value contains it, which `applytotext`'s longest-first order then handles. The relation is transitive and antisymmetric, so survivors are the maximal elements and no pair can shadow each other into both being dropped. **Spanless entities are explicitly out of scope and cannot be made order-independent**: the regex layer sets `start:0;end:0` on everything (PL1.7), so every span is identical and empty; requiring a non-empty span on both sides means such an entity neither shadows nor is shadowed, which is byte-for-byte the previous outcome. 41 assertions, 7 of them red against the unmodified pipeline. *Amended 2026-09-23:* PL1.7 has since landed and the regex layer reports real spans, so shadowing is live for it and the spanless carve-out described here now covers the two NER layers only. The 41 assertions are unaffected and still pass — that test builds its spans by hand and never calls the detector |
| PL1.10 | M | **Done** | **`patautfn` and `patauabn` were unanchored, so every Australian mobile was also a Tax File Number** — Found 2026-09-23 by AD1.8, the first time the privacy harness had ever run. `patautfn` matches three groups of three digits, so it matched the trailing nine digits of "0412 345 678": the phone case reported **two** entities and the sensitivity escalated CONFIDENTIAL → **RESTRICTED**. `patauabn` did the same to any 11-digit run. Over-detection is fail-safe for redaction and **not** for sensitivity — that label drives routing and gating, so a phone number silently promoted a request to the most restricted tier. Fixed by a boundary check rejecting a match with a digit immediately either side, applied to the seven bare-digit-run patterns, **inside the detection loop before the sensitivity maximum is taken** so a rejected match cannot escalate the label. It could not be done with a regex anchor: toke's engine has no `\b` and exposes **no match offset at all**, so it needed PL1.7's spans first. `au_tfn` is additionally gated on its real weighted-modulus checksum. *Done 2026-09-23. **Measured, both ways:** precision 0.700 → **0.894**, false positives 36 → **10**, recall byte-identical on all three definitions — 26 of 36 false positives removed at zero recall cost. Recorded in `docs/metrics-baseline.md` as a second dated block rather than by overwriting the first, because the 2026-09-19 figures are what earlier claims were made against. The corpus generator was also corrected: it built the dash-separated adversarial TFN by bumping a valid number's last digit, breaking the checksum while still labelling it `AU_TFN, expected_detected: true` — so 4 of 16 positives asserted that a non-TFN must be detected as one, which is exactly what would have made checksum gating look like a 25% recall loss. `ssn_us` is deliberately not checksum-gated (its rules are not a single modulus), which is why 10 false positives remain. Remaining tail: `au_tfn` still fires inside `au_abn`, where the boundary check cannot help because the neighbouring characters are spaces.* |
| PL1.5 | S | **Done** | **Correct the documents that assume a property the code lacks** — `~/tk/toke-website/templates/loke.tkt:51` publicly claims "consistent placeholders"; `docs/specifications/regulatory-defaults.md:99` reasons about whether consistent placeholders meet the Recital 26 threshold; `packages/core/src/privacy/placeholder_store.tkc.md:1` claims restoration "across sessions" when the store is scoped `WHERE request_id=?` with 24-hour expiry. All three describe behaviour that does not exist. |
| PL1.6 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **The local NER layer is unreachable, and would drop its own results if it ran** — Found 2026-09-23 (PL1.1). Three defects in `core.privacy.ner_local`: (a) `nerlocalparseentities` evaluates `mt items {...}` and **discards it**, then returns the original `entities`, so a successful parse drops the entity — the same discarded-value class as the old `addentry`; (b) it handles a single `item` rather than looping the array, so at most one entity could ever be parsed; (c) the whole file types entity lists as `@i64`, erasing the element type. And it does not matter yet, which is the fourth defect: **`pipeline.run` never calls `ner_local` at all** — only the Ollama-hosted `core.privacy.ner`. So loke ships a local NER layer that no request path can reach. Decide whether it is a layer or dead code, then either wire it through the filter registry and fix (a)–(c), or delete it and withdraw any claim that depends on it<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24 — decided: dead code, deleted.** Verified before deleting: (i) no module imports `core.privacy.nerlocal` (grep over `packages/`, `src/`, `tests/`; only an archived test and comments); (ii) its in-process path sits on `core.models.infer` over `std.infer`, which is a **stub** in toke (T-11: `isloaded` is always false, `generate` returns a sentinel), so the path could never run; (iii) its only other branch called `core.privacy.ner` with a hardcoded URL — a duplicate of the layer the pipeline already calls. So (a)–(c) are moot rather than fixed. No claim depended on it: claims.md A3 cites `ner.tk`, not this file. `docs/privacy-filters.md` and `docs/test-coverage.md` updated; build green without it. There is no failing test to show for a deletion; the evidence is the absence above. Writing this found that the layer A3 **does** cite detects nothing either — PL1.12 — and A3 is downgraded to `partial`. |
| PL1.7 | S | **Done** | *Done 2026-09-23.* **The regex layer supplies no spans, so overlap resolution cannot work on it** — Found 2026-09-23 (PL1.1). `regex.rundetection` set `start:0;end:0` on **every** entity it emitted, so `strictlyshadows` compared the same empty span every time and shadowing never fired for the only detection layer that runs without a network sidecar. **This story's own premise was wrong and is corrected here:** toke's regex engine exposes **no match offset anywhere** — `str.findall`, `str.find`, `str.matches`, `str.countre` and `str.replacere` are all typed `(i64,i64)->i64` in the stdlib interface and none returns a position — so "the regex engine has them" was not true. The offsets are recovered from the matched **value** instead: `str.indexof` over the remainder of the text from a cursor that advances past each match, which relies on `str.findall` returning matches left-to-right (measured, on a 19-digit run). A value occurring twice therefore gets two distinct spans rather than the first one twice — measured on `"0412 345 678 and again 0412 345 678"`, now spans 0–12 and 23–35. A value that cannot be located from the cursor keeps the old `0/0` rather than being given an invented offset, because a fabricated span would silently mis-drive dedup. Shadowing is now live for this layer; the spanless carve-out documented in PL1.9 covers the two NER layers only. 41 assertions in `tests/unit/privacy/test_regex_spans.tk`. The 41 in `test_placeholder_collision.tk` stay green **unchanged**, because that test constructs its spans by hand and never calls the detector |
| PL1.8 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **Presidio confidence scores read as zero** — Found 2026-09-23 (PL1.1). `presidio.parseentityitem` reads `score` with `json.i64` into an `f64` field, so a score of 0.92 arrives as 0. Currently harmless only because `mapconfidence` is used instead and `.score` is never read — which is its own smell, since the field exists, is populated wrongly, and is load-bearing for AD1.3's per-layer marginal value and for any confidence threshold in `$layerconfig`. Same family as LS1.1i, in a file that sweep did not cover<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24.** `parseentityitem` reads `score` with `json.f64`, falling back to `0.0` on `$jsonerr` (which std.json also reports for a literal `0`, T-33, so the fallback is exact). `tests/unit/privacy/test_presidio_parse.tk` (new) asserts 0.92, 0.55 and 1.0 arrive as themselves and that a missing score reads 0.0. **Mutation-checked:** with `json.i64` restored, four assertions go red. Still not done by this row: `topiientities` assigns confidence from `mapconfidence(entitytype)` and ignores `.score`; whether the real score should replace the per-type constant is AD1.3's decision, not taken here. And the field could not have been read at all before PL1.11 — see there. |
| PL1.11 | S | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **The Presidio client could not parse any non-error answer** — Found 2026-09-24 writing PL1.8's test. `/analyze` answers with a top-level JSON array, and `presidio.parseentities` called `json.getarr(arr;"")`, which looks up the key `""` in an object: std.json's key scan returns not-found for text that does not begin with `{`, `getarr` returns a null array handle, and `items.len` reads address -8. **The first line of the new test crashed with SIGSEGV against the unfixed code.** So with `use_presidio` on and a sidecar up, every request that Presidio answered took the process down; nothing noticed because no test and no deployment has ever run the sidecar (claims.md A4). Fixed with `json.parsearray` plus a per-element `json.dec`, iterated with the null-safe `str.arraylen`; `[]` parses to zero entities. The same `json.getarr(arr;"")` call is in `core.privacy.ner` and `core.privacy.ner_local` — filed as PL1.12 for the former; the latter is PL1.6. |
| PL1.12 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **The Ollama NER layer detects nothing and reports itself healthy** — Found 2026-09-24 (PL1.11), and **executed, not inferred**: a probe test against the real `core.privacy.ner` got **zero** entities from `parsecontent("[{\"type\":\"PERSON\",\"value\":\"Alice\"}]")` and zero from `parseresponse` on a well-formed Ollama `/api/chat` body. Three defects, each sufficient: (1) `buildentities` calls `json.getarr(arr;"")` on a top-level array, which is the PL1.11 null-handle defect — here it does not crash only because the loop uses the null-safe `str.arraylen`, so it silently yields nothing; (2) `parseobj` reads `json.str(obj;"message.content")`, and std.json's key scan has no dotted-path support, so `content` is empty for every response; (3) `detectentities` splices the user's prompt into the request JSON unescaped, so any prompt containing a quote or newline sends malformed JSON. `pipeline.run` counts the stage as **completed** with 0 entities, so with `use_ner` on the pipeline believes a second detection layer ran when it contributed nothing — the fail-closed count in NC1.9 is inflated by a layer that cannot detect. Fix all three and assert entity recovery from a canned Ollama body; the sidecar round trip needs a separate test.<br><br>**Done 2026-09-24.** All three fixed: the reply is read with `json.parsearray` and a per-element `json.dec`; `message` is fetched with `json.getval` and `content` read from it in a second step; the request body is built by `requestbody`, which escapes the prompt with `shared.jsonobj.escape` (std.json's `json.enc` leaves newlines raw, T-45), and the system message now names the `type`/`value` fields the parser reads. `tests/unit/privacy/test_ner_parse.tk` (new). **Mutation-checked:** against the old parse and body code, 5 assertions and the guarded branch go red. Not covered: a live Ollama round trip, and whether a given model follows the requested format. claims.md A3 stays `partial` for that reason. |

---

# VERIFICATION PROGRAMME

> **Why this layer exists**
>
> loke publishes specific numbers — token reduction, pipeline overhead, classification latency,
> compression ratio, retrieval latency, cache hit rate — and none of them is produced by a
> measurement. The methodology, however, already exists and is genuinely rigorous:
> `docs/research/toon-benchmark-methodology.md` specifies named baselines, twelve task types, four
> data shapes, a licensed dataset table, N with warm-up discard, BCa bootstrap confidence intervals,
> Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, Benjamini-Hochberg correction, Cliff's delta, SHA-256 frozen snapshots
> and judge calibration against human annotation. `docs/research/research-proposal.md` §5.2 specifies
> per-entity precision/recall/F1 and marginal recall per added detection layer.
>
> The task is therefore **implementation, not design**. One thing must be built first: `std.test`
> exposes three assertion functions over string comparison and cannot record a numeric result, so
> today a test can prove a threshold was met but cannot publish the value it measured.

## Epic VM1: Verification Measurement Substrate

*Build the apparatus that lets a measurement be recorded, repeated and reproduced. Prerequisite for every other story in this layer.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| VM1.1 | M | **Done** | **Numeric result sink** — A structured results file (one JSON object per measurement) carrying the value, units, N, the workload identifier, the tokenizer where relevant, the model and its pinned version, the seed, the timestamp and the toolchain commit from `TOOLCHAIN.lock`. `std.test` cannot carry a number, so measurement runs must write results directly rather than asserting through the test harness. |
| VM1.2 | S | **Done** | **Pin what is currently unpinned** — `packages/privacy-filter/server.py:34-38` loads `openai/privacy-filter` with no revision, so the model is whatever the hub resolves at first run. Pin the revision. Record every external model's exact version string in each result. |
| VM1.3 | M | **Partial** | **Repetition and intervals** — Run each configuration N times with the first run discarded as warm-up, and report BCa bootstrap confidence intervals, exactly as `docs/research/toon-benchmark-methodology.md` already specifies. No single-run number may be published.<br><br>**Status detail:** Partial — repetition with warm-up discard and bootstrap intervals are implemented in `benchmarks/lib/result.py`, and a repeated measurement with no interval is rejected. **The interval is a percentile bootstrap, not the BCa the methodology specifies**, and says so rather than claiming BCa |
| VM1.4 | M | **Done** | **Containerised harness** — No Dockerfile exists anywhere in the repo. Add a pinned container so a measurement can be reproduced by someone else, and record the image digest in each result. |
| VM1.5 | S | **Done** | **Numbers out of CI** — `.github/workflows/ci.yml` uploads one artefact containing pass/fail counts only. Add a second artefact carrying measurement results, so a number can be traced to the commit that produced it. |
| VM1.6 | S |  | **Repair and wire the existing harness** — `packages/core/src/eval/bench.tk` is a working keyword-scoring benchmark with no callers, no persistence, and loop bounds that read one element past the end (`:85`, `:128`, `:171`). Give it an entry point, persist through VM1.1, and add the `loke benchmark` subcommand missing from `packages/cli/src/commands.tk`. |
| VM1.7 | M | **Partial** | **Implement the TOON methodology** — Build `benchmarks/toon/` to the specification already written in `docs/research/toon-benchmark-methodology.md`: baselines B1 (raw JSON, primary), B2 (minified), B3 (YAML); the named dataset table with licence checks and SHA-256 snapshots; token counting per provider tokenizer rather than the character-length ratio `optimiser/toon.tk:282` currently computes.<br><br>**Status detail:** **Partial — baselines done, and they matter.** [`benchmarks/toon/`](../benchmarks/toon/README.md) measures B1/B2/B3 with real tokenisers (tiktoken, offline) through the result sink; 7 self-test guard groups in the gate. The finding is about the baseline rather than any technique: **whitespace removal alone is worth 1.35x-1.80x**, so a figure quoted against pretty-printed JSON is mostly measuring indentation, and YAML is worse than minified JSON on every payload. Published in [`metrics-baseline.md`](metrics-baseline.md). **TOON itself is not measured** — no faithful encoder exists outside toke, and one written from the format's description would measure this harness's own invention under TOON's name. Anthropic and Google token counts are absent, not approximated. The accuracy half needs model spend (decision M) |
| VM1.8 | S | **Done** | **Single source for published numbers** — Create `docs/metrics-baseline.md` as the only file the project publishes figures from, each with its tokenizer or "bytes", its N and its date; and adopt the precedence rule that any other page disagreeing with it is a bug. Publish negative results there too. |

## Epic CB1: Correctness Benchmark — Three Arms

*Prove that a no-custody architecture can do the work, or find out that it cannot. Depends on VM1 and research spike RS3.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| CB1.1 | M | **Done** | **Define the three arms, and the honest framing** — (A) data in the prompt; (B) schema only, model answers directly; (C) schema only, model returns an artifact, loke executes locally. State which model does what in each arm: arm C does not use a *local* model for generation at all, only for custody, which answers the "handicapped by weak local models" objection. **Framing constraint:** the arm-C data flow is not novel — it is how every text-to-SQL benchmark already works and it ships in several commercial products (`docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §10). This epic measures the **cost** of strict no-custody, candidly, and should be expected to lose ground on some question classes; it does not claim the architecture as an invention. |
| CB1.2 | L |  | **Execution-based scoring harness** — Primary benchmark: **InfiAgent-DABench** (257 closed-form questions over 52 CSV files, Apache-2.0, Python sandbox), because it is the only candidate where paste-the-data is the honest status-quo baseline; on the text-to-SQL suites schema-only is already the norm, so measuring there proves nothing. Score by executing the result with a gold-executability invariant asserted first. **257 items gives roughly ±6 pp binomial confidence, so comparisons must be paired on identical items and tested with McNemar, not as two independent proportions.** Secondary: BIRD Mini-Dev (500 items), noting its efficiency metric is computed only over queries that pass execution accuracy and is therefore not comparable across systems with different accuracy. **Avoid Spider 2.0-Snow** — audited annotation-error rate 62.8–66.1%. |
| CB1.2a | M |  | **Freeze the output-contract reformat step — largest validity threat** — DABench post-processes model output into its answer template with a separate reformat model, and that step is worth **up to 32 accuracy points** (Mistral-7B 6.23 → 38.67; GPT-4 72.76 → 78.99). That is larger than any plausible effect of the disclosure regime under test. If arm C returns a structured spec (naturally parseable) and arm A returns prose with an embedded answer, the experiment measures format compliance and calls it privacy-preserving correctness. The reformat step must be **identical, frozen and applied symmetrically to every arm**, and both reformat-on and reformat-off numbers reported. |
| CB1.3 | M | **Done** | **Output-contract control** — Adherence to the required output format materially changes scores on published analysis benchmarks, so the contract must be treated as a controlled variable and reported, not left implicit. |
| CB1.4 | M |  | **Pre-empt the corrected-benchmark critique** — Published benchmarks carry large annotation-error rates (BIRD Mini-Dev 52.8%; Spider 2.0-Snow 62.8–66.1%), moving scores by −7% to 31% and rankings by up to nine positions across 16 re-evaluated agents. Cite the journal version (arXiv:2601.08778 = PVLDB 19(5)), not only the workshop precursor. **loke's cheaper and stronger pre-emption:** because the comparison is within-benchmark and paired — same question, same data, two disclosure regimes — annotation errors are a *shared* confound that largely cancels in the difference. Say so, then report paired deltas rather than leaderboard absolutes, hand-audit a stratified sample of ~100 items, publish the audit, and report raw and audited subsets separately. |
| CB1.5 | M |  | **Cost and token accounting per arm** — Tokens per completed task against arm A as the named baseline. This is what makes "minimising token spend" falsifiable; today no baseline workload is defined anywhere. |
| CB1.6 | M |  | **Position against the closest prior art on its own datasets** — The nearest work formalises minimisation as an ordinal lattice over per-entity actions (retain, abstract, redact) and searches it under a utility floor, but **every point in that lattice still sends data to the model**. loke's no-custody path sits outside it, made feasible by moving the computation rather than the data (`docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §5). That is a real contribution **only if** loke demonstrates comparable utility at a comparable operating point **on the same datasets** that work uses; anything less reads as redacting everything and losing the task. Also reuse its two black-box recovery audits verbatim so loke's numbers are directly comparable to a published baseline. Schema-only prompting is already standard in text-to-SQL — cite it as support, do not deny it. Depends on RS4. |

## Epic AD1: Adversarial Corpora

*Ground truth for detection, and real attacks for enforcement. None of this exists today.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| AD1.1 | L | **Done** | **PII ground-truth corpus** — No labelled corpus exists in the repo and no public dataset is referenced anywhere in code. Build or import one in the span format `[{text, entities:[{start,end,type}]}]` that `docs/epics-and-stories.md` F3b.9 already specifies, with a documented matching rule (exact span versus overlap — currently unspecified anywhere) and locale coverage including Australian identifier types. |
| AD1.2 | M | **Partial** | **Entity-level scoring with a recall floor** — False negatives leak identity, so recall is the decisive metric: set a high floor on direct identifiers and report precision as a utility cost, not as the headline. `packages/core/src/privacy/evaluation.tk` already implements correct precision/recall/F1 with zero-division guards but has no callers and no data source — wire it.<br><br>**Status detail:** Partial — regex layer measured (`metrics-baseline.md`); the compiled detector and the recall floor need the toolchain |
| AD1.3 | M |  | **Marginal value per detection layer** — Report what each added layer contributes in recall and what it costs in false positives, as `docs/research/research-proposal.md` §5.2 already specifies. This is the evidence that determines whether the multi-layer design earns its complexity. |
| AD1.4 | M | **Partial** — sidecar applies and reports the threshold; the toke client still does not pass one | **Operating-point control** — The sidecar's `confidence_threshold` is declared in `packages/privacy-filter/models.json` and **never applied**: `/detect` and `/anonymise` return every classifier span regardless of score. Without it there is no operating point to tune and no precision/recall curve to report. Plumb it through `packages/core/src/privacy/sidecar_client.tk`, which currently returns unparsed response strings with no timeout or retry. |
| AD1.5 | L |  | **Injection and exfiltration corpus** — Single-turn injections now yield near-zero attack success on frontier models while a persistent multi-step attack reaches **95.5%** on the same model, so a single-turn corpus scores misleadingly well. Build around loke's own invariants and the four attack families in `docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §14: outbound disclosure (including the iterative query-oracle class of NC1.10), artifact abuse with parser-differential variants between validator and executor, data-flow exfiltration, and **persistence** — injections landing in cached schema descriptions, column comments, saved queries or few-shot examples and firing on a later request. If loke caches schemas or learns from prior queries, persistence is the highest-severity untested class. **Every attack case needs a paired benign twin**, and every configuration must report a 4-tuple: benign utility, utility under attack, attack success rate, and **false-block rate** — over-refusal is the failure mode a schema-enforcing proxy is most exposed to. Define the acceptance gate as a composite up front. Add an adaptive tier: publish the rules, let a red team optimise against them for a fixed budget, report static and adaptive separately. Extend the published data-flow exfiltration methodology rather than reinventing it. |
| AD1.6 | M | **Done** | **Enumerated bypass corpus — six classes** — Each has vendor or RFC authority and is independently testable (`docs/research/disclosure-measurement-findings.md` §14): (1) default TLS-interception do-not-decrypt exemptions, shipped enabled — test whether any provider endpoint falls in one; (2) certificate pinning in native apps and SDKs, where the documented remedy is *deliberate* loss of visibility; (3) split tunnelling, including URL-based exclusion shipped as a browser extension — test whether a user can exclude an API host without admin rights; (4) browser extensions, both as a bypass vector and as the only enforcement point surviving encrypted transport metadata; (5) **encrypted transport metadata — RFC 9849 TLS Encrypted Client Hello, Standards Track, March 2026, with OpenSSL support shipped**, against which SNI-based rules fail *silently*, plus QUIC/HTTP-3 and DNS-over-HTTPS; (6) direct-to-API egress with the caller's own credentials, for which **no vendor documents a reliable network-layer control**. loke additionally ships `--no-privacy` (`locales/en.json:390`) and its proxy requires opt-in client reconfiguration rather than intercepting anything. Publish as "enforced at N of 6, with these requiring endpoint or managed-policy co-deployment" — never as unbypassability. |
| AD1.7 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-24 | **Wire Epic F3b's orphaned modules or withdraw the claim** — Ten multi-layer privacy modules have zero importers: `filter_registry`, `layer_config`, `consensus`, `filter_metrics`, `evaluation`, `sidecar_client`, `layer_health`, `entity_routing`, `org_policy`, `test_harness`. `privacy/pipeline.tk` imports none of them and uses its own `dedupentities()`. `consensus.tk:24 resolveoverlaps` ignores its `strategy` argument entirely and marks every detection `action:"mask"`, so the four named strategies exist only as display strings. ## Epic GA5: Assurance Evidence

*Make the audit trail something an assessor could actually rely on, and be precise about what it does and does not evidence.*

| Story | Size | Status | Summary |
|-------|------|--------|---------|
| GA5.1 | S | **Done** | **Fix audit timestamps** — `packages/core/src/storage/audit.tk:47` writes the **string literal** `'now()'` into `created_at`, so every row carries the same seven characters instead of a time. `ORDER BY created_at` is therefore undefined and the period filters in `packages/core/src/governance/dashboard.tk:63,75,86,97` cannot work. Without a time basis there is no period, and without a period there is no evidence of a control operating *over* one.<br><br>**Status detail:** **Done** — `created_at` is a real epoch-ms from `db.nowms`, and the chain-tail query orders `created_at DESC, rowid DESC` so same-millisecond rows still order deterministically |
| GA5.2 | M | **Done** | **Real hash chain** — `audit.tk:38` computes `hash = str.concat(correlationid, eventtype)`. That is not a hash, it covers none of the payload, and `prev_hash` is stored but is **not an input**, so nothing chains. Compute a cryptographic digest over a canonical serialisation of every persisted field **including the previous row's hash**, using an unambiguous field separator and a defined genesis value. toke provides `crypto.sha256`, `crypto.tohex` and `crypto.constanteq`.<br><br>**Status detail:** **Done** — `eventdigest(prevhash;id;event;created)` sha256s the previous hash plus all fourteen fields. Tamper and gap detection are still unasserted: that is GA5.3 |
| GA5.3 | M | **Done** | *Done 2026-09-23.* **Chain verification** — `audit.verifychain(store)` reads FORWARD in insertion order, checks each row's `prev_hash` against the previous row's `hash`, and recomputes each digest. Returns a plain `$chainresult` struct, never a result: T-18 would make a `$err` arm dead, and a verifier whose failure reads as "chain intact" is the worst available outcome for this particular function. Reports the first divergence, because everything after it is downstream of the same break. **A real bug surfaced while writing it and changed the design:** the verifier first rebuilt an `$auditevent` from the stored columns and re-ran `eventdigest`, and an intact chain did not verify — `cost_usd` is written with `str.fromfloat` and read back through hundredths as `0.01` where `0.005` went in, so the recomputed digest differed on every row. The chain was fine; the reconstruction was lossy. `eventdigest` is now split so the writer and the verifier hash the **same joined field string**, and the verifier reads every column as TEXT. A digest over a re-parsed value is a digest over a guess about the parser. Acceptance met in `tests/integration/test_audit_trail_e2e.tk`: an intact chain verifies, and a mutated field, a mutated digest and a **deleted row** are each detected — every tamper applied with raw SQL, deliberately going around the module under test, because a test that can only tamper through the API it tests proves nothing about an attacker with database access |
| GA5.4 | S | Blocked — F10.12 | **Delete the duplicate implementation** — `src/core/audit/trail.tk` is worse than the live one: `chainhash()` returns `str.fromi32(str.len(acc))` (a string length), `verifychain()` is hardcoded `<true`, and `newevent()` sets `let now:i64=0`. It has no callers and has never compiled. Remove it rather than fixing it.<br><br>**Status detail:** Blocked — file carries uncommitted migration edits; belongs with the legacy `src/` removal in **F10.12** |
| GA5.5 | M | **Done** — 2026-09-26 | **Persist the decision trace** — `packages/core/src/governance/trace.tk`'s `$decisiontrace` has seventeen well-chosen fields, no caller, no table and no insert; `complete()` writes a single log line and discards the structure. Persist it, and add the fields an assessor needs that exist nowhere in `$auditevent`: policy decision, entities detected, detection layer, approval or override outcome, and kill-switch state. **Done 2026-09-26.** This finishes the disclosure worker's parked work (`work/da-wip`), which stopped at an account usage limit. Migration 30 creates `decision_trace`, with one row per decision, written by `core.governance.trace.persist`. The request path records every outcome: completed, provider failure, privacy refusal and kill-switch refusal. **What is stored is the decision, not the conversation.** There is no column for the prompt, the anonymised prompt, the response or the restored output, because the database is plaintext on disk (X8) and storing them would copy the protected data into a weaker store. Entities are stored as types and counts. Stored: the outcome; the policy decision and risk tier, which were declared and never set before; the detection layers scanned and skipped; the approval outcome (`required_not_sought` where GA5.26 applies); the kill-switch state; the model and provider; the anonymisation and restoration flags; the duration and the step count. The four assessor fields live in a separate `$assessorfields`, because toke caps a struct at sixteen fields (T-53, renumbered from the worker's T-46). **Evidence:** `tests/integration/test_decision_trace.tk`, 19 assertions, including that the email and card number from the request appear in NO column. With `persist` disabled, 16 of them failed. |

RAISE(ABORT)`. `core.storage.db.auditappendonlyenforced()` checks `sqlite_master` for both by name rather than assuming a migration that ran once is still in effect — a restored backup would carry its triggers, but an operator with the sqlite3 shell can DROP them. `core.governance.compliance` reports the true state either way, and its two messages are worth quoting because they set the limit honestly: enforcement *"stops modification over any ordinary connection; it does not stop someone who can DROP TRIGGER first, which is what the hash chain covers"*. | GA5.10 | M | **Done** | **The DSAR export payload is garbage for every table it embeds** — Found 2026-09-23 (LS1.1i). `governance/dsar.tk:96` calls `json.enc(row)`. `std.json` declares `json.enc(str):str` — it is a **string escaper** — and it is being handed a `$row` handle, which compiles because the ABI erases both to `i64`, the same class as T-23. `rowstojson` is generic over an arbitrary `SELECT`, so this is not an accessor swap: serialising a row needs its column names, which the function does not have. Either pass the column list alongside the SQL, or return typed rows per table and serialise each explicitly. A subject access request returning malformed JSON is a compliance defect, not a cosmetic one *Confirmed 2026-09-23 while fixing the rest of `dsar.tk`, and deliberately left: it needs a per-table column map, and `SELECT *` gives unknown columns with no `db.columns` binding in toke to enumerate them. **The DSAR access export is not trustworthy** — every per-table array is whatever `json.enc` makes of a row pointer. This is now the largest remaining hole in that file, and `dsar.tk`'s other defects (the absent `audit_events` table, an erasure that would be rejected by the new append-only trigger, a `WHERE user_id=?` against a table with no `user_id`) are fixed around it.* **Done 2026-09-24, and the row understates it.** The `json.enc(row)` half is fixed as the row prescribes: `exportcolumns(table)` gives each exported table an explicit column list, the `SELECT` names its columns instead of `*` (so the payload does not depend on table creation order), and `rowjson` emits `{\"col\":\"value\"}` reading every column with `s.rowstr`. Every value is a JSON STRING including the INTEGER and REAL columns, deliberately: `rowstr` is the one accessor correct for every column type, and a number read with the wrong accessor is a zero, which in a compliance export is a false claim.<br><br>**What the row did not name, and it is worse than what it did.** TWO of the four exported tables have no `user_id` column at all — `quota_usage` (id, rule_id, scope, period_key, used_tokens, used_cost_usd) and `quality_signals` (id, request_id, model_id, provider, use_case, thumbs_up, coherence_score, relevance_score, created_at). `SELECT * FROM quota_usage WHERE user_id=?` is a statement with no referent; SQLite rejected it, the `$err` arm called `exportmissing`, which logged **\"no such table\" for a table that exists**, and the subject was handed `[]` — told loke holds no such records about them when the truth is that the question cannot be asked. Both are string comparisons of `"1758…"` against `"2026-…"`, so **every window matches zero rows** — the governance reports and the scorecard period are inert regardless of what the table holds. Needs a date-to-epoch-ms conversion at the query boundary, and a test that asserts a row inside the window is returned and one outside it is not **Done, verified 2026-09-24 rather than taken on trust.** All three subjects go through `core.storage.db`: `report-engine.tk` resolves the filter dates with `msrangeof` and builds its predicate from `createdatwindowclause`; `dashboard.tk` resolves its period with `daysagoms` and binds it against `CAST(created_at AS INTEGER)`; `report.tk` uses the same clause. One spelling of the predicate — `CAST(created_at AS INTEGER) >= ? AND CAST(created_at AS INTEGER) < ?` — so the five report queries and the scorecard cannot drift apart again. `msrangeof` converts through SQLite's own `strftime`, so the clock that writes the column is the clock that reads it, and it REFUSES three bad inputs rather than matching nothing: an unreadable start date, an unreadable end date, and an end before the start — `strftime` returns NULL for a date it cannot read, which arrives as 0, and a bound of 0 silently matches nothing, which is the defect this row is about appearing in the fix for it.<br><br>**And it is tested on membership, not on shape,** in `tests/unit/governance/test_report_window.tk` (21 assertions against the real report engine): the exact epoch millisecond of both bounds; a row inside the window returned while the row outside it is not; a row AT the exclusive end bound excluded; an event in the final millisecond of the end date included; an empty window returning no rows. A test asserting only that the query ran, or that the report has a title, would have passed throughout the defect. |
